<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:intro-midpoint}
Assume \eqref{eq:sc-smooth} and \(0<\varepsilon\leqslant\sqrt d/2\). Then, Heun initialized at the minimizer
\(x_\star\) attains \eqref{eq:global-absolute-criterion} with
\[
    {\widetilde O\bigl(\kappa^{4/3}d^{1/3}\varepsilon^{-2/3}\bigr)}
\]
gradient queries. Underdamped exponential midpoint with friction
\(2\sqrt\beta\), initialized with \(X_0=x_\star\) and independent
\(P_0\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I_d)\), attains the same position accuracy with
\[
    {\widetilde O\bigl(\kappa^{5/4}d^{1/4}\varepsilon^{-1/2}\bigr)}
\]
gradient queries. Here \(\widetilde O\) suppresses logarithmic factors.
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:intro-lower}
For each method in \Cref{tab:intro-comparison},
and every sufficiently small normalized step \(h\), there exist a
strongly convex, smooth target with bounded condition number and an
initial distribution satisfying
\[
 \mathbb{E}[V(X_0)-V(x_\star)]\leqslant Cd\,,
 \qquad x_\star\coloneqq\operatorname*{arg\,min} V\,,
\]
such that
for every sufficiently small \(\varepsilon>0\), achieving
\(\sqrt\alpha\,W_2(\nu_N,\pi)\leqslant\varepsilon\) requires at least
the query complexity in~\Cref{tab:intro-comparison}'s lower bound column, with logarithmic
factors suppressed. Here \(\nu_N\) is the position distribution after
\(N\) steps. In the underdamped case, this initial distribution is on $X_0$ (with \(P_0\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I_d)\) independent of
\(X_0\)).

The stationary bounds are stated in
\Cref{prop:heun-oscillatory-lower,prop:exponential-midpoint-Gaussian-lower,prop:overdamped-common-construction,prop:randomized-underdamped-Gaussian-lower}.
The finite iteration bounds and their initialization conditions are in
\Cref{cor:midpoint-iteration-lower};
\Cref{thm:et-linear-kappa-lower} additionally quantifies the condition
number dependence for Heun.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
\label{rem:friction-choice}
We choose \(\gamma=2\sqrt\beta\), following the synchronous coupling
analysis of underdamped Langevin in
\cite[Theorem~5.3.6]{ChewiBook}.  This choice permits contraction in a
fixed norm combining position and momentum.  It becomes \(\gamma=2\)
under the normalization in \Cref{sec:normalization}.  Our results do not
address discretizations with smaller friction.
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:et-upper}
There are universal constants \(c,C>0\) such that, if
\(0<\beta h\leqslant h_0\), then \(K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}}\) has a unique
invariant distribution \(\widehat\pi_h^{\mathrm{Heun}}\) and
\begin{equation}
    \alphaW_2^2(\widehat\pi_h^{\mathrm{Heun}},\pi)
    \leqslant C\kappa d\,(\beta h)^3\,.
    \label{eq:et-stationary-upper}
\end{equation}
For every \(\mu\in\mathcal P_2(\mathbb{R}^d)\) and integer \(N\geqslant0\),
\begin{equation}
    \alphaW_2^2(\mu (K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})^N,\pi)
    \leqslant C\mathrm{e}^{-cN\beta h/\kappa}\alphaW_2^2(\mu,\pi)
       +C\kappa d\,(\beta h)^3\,.
    \label{eq:et-finite-time-upper}
\end{equation}
In particular, for every
\(\varepsilon>0\), the number of gradient queries sufficient
for \eqref{eq:global-absolute-criterion} is at most
\[
    C\,\Bigl(\kappa+
       \frac{\kappa^{4/3}d^{1/3}}{\varepsilon^{2/3}}\Bigr)
       \log\Bigl(\mathrm{e}+\frac{\alphaW_2^2(\mu,\pi)}{\varepsilon^2}\Bigr)\,.
\]
Initialization at the minimizer \(x_\star\) satisfies
\(\alpha W_2^2(\delta_{x_\star},\pi) \leqslant d\),
and hence, for \(0<\varepsilon\leqslant\sqrt d/2\), gives
\begin{equation}
    N_{\nabla V}\leqslant C\,{\frac{\kappa^{4/3}d^{1/3}}{\varepsilon^{2/3}}}
       \log\frac d{\varepsilon^2}\,.
    \label{eq:et-complexity}
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:et-contractivity}
For \(0<h\leqslant1\) and all \(\mu,\nu\in\mathcal P_2(\mathbb{R}^d)\),
\begin{equation}
    W_2(\mu K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}},\nu K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})
    \leqslant\rhoW_2(\mu,\nu)\,,
    \qquad \rho\leqslant1-\frac h{2\kappa}\,.
    \label{eq:et-contraction-factor}
\end{equation}
In particular, \(K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}}\) has a unique invariant
 distribution in \(\mathcal P_2(\mathbb{R}^d)\).
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:et-local-framework}
Let \(0<h\leqslant1\), let \(\rho\) be as in
\eqref{eq:et-contraction-factor}, and let
\(\nu\in\mathcal P_2(\mathbb{R}^d)\).  Then
\begin{equation}
    W_2(\widehat\pi_h^{\mathrm{Heun}},\nu)
    \leqslantW_2(\nu K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}},\nu)
       +\frac{W_2\bigl(\nu(K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})^2,
          \nu K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}}\bigr)}{1-\rho}\,.
    \label{eq:et-local-framework}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rem:et-condition-gap}
The bound \eqref{eq:et-complexity} contains
\(\kappa^{4/3}d^{1/3}\varepsilon^{-2/3}\).  The lower bound in
\Cref{thm:et-linear-kappa-lower} contains
\(\kappa d^{1/3}\varepsilon^{-2/3}\) for
\(d^{-1}\leqslant\varepsilon\leqslant\varepsilon_0\), so the exponents of \(d\) and
\(\varepsilon\) agree while a factor \(\kappa^{1/3}\) remains.
In the upper bound, this loss comes from accumulating the distance
between consecutive iterates by the triangle inequality.  Improving
that accumulation would require further cancellation across steps;
the present proof does not establish it.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:uld-midpoint-main}
There are universal constants \(c,C>0\) such that, for
\(0<\sqrt\beta\,h\leqslant{c\kappa^{-1/4}}\), the kernel \(K_h^{\mathrm{ExpMid}}\)
has a unique invariant distribution \(\bm\pi_h^{\mathrm{ExpMid}}\) in
\(\mathcal P_2(\mathbb{R}^{2d})\), and
\begin{equation}
    \sqrt\alpha\,W_2
       \bigl((\bm\pi_h^{\mathrm{ExpMid}})_x,\pi\bigr)
    \leqslant C\sqrt{\kappa d}\,(\sqrt\beta\,h)^2\,.
    \label{eq:uld-midpoint-position-bias}
\end{equation}
For the initialization \(\widehat X_0=x_\star\) and
\(\widehat P_0\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I_d)\), independent of the driving Brownian
motion, for every integer
\(n\geqslant0\),
\begin{equation}
    \sqrt\alpha\,W_2
       \bigl(\operatorname{Law}(\widehat X_n),\pi\bigr)
    \leqslant C\sqrt d\,\mathrm{e}^{-cn\sqrt\beta\,h/\kappa}
       +C\sqrt{\kappa d}\,(\sqrt\beta\,h)^2\,.
    \label{eq:uld-midpoint-finite-time-explicit}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for \(0<\varepsilon\leqslant\sqrt d/2\), the number
\(N_{\nabla V}\) of gradient queries required to attain the
accuracy criterion \eqref{eq:global-absolute-criterion} satisfies
\begin{equation}
    N_{\nabla V}\leqslant{C\,\frac{\kappa^{5/4}d^{1/4}}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}\,
       \log\frac d{\varepsilon^2}}\,.
    \label{eq:uld-midpoint-complexity}
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:uld-midpoint-numerical-contraction}
There is a universal constant \(c>0\) such that, for
\(0<h\leqslant{c\kappa^{-1/4}}\) and all
\(\mu,\nu\in\mathcal P_2(\mathbb{R}^{2d})\),
\[
    W_{2,M}(\mu K_h^{\mathrm{ExpMid}},\nu K_h^{\mathrm{ExpMid}})
    \leqslant\Bigl(1-\frac{h}{4\kappa}\Bigr)\,
       W_{2,M}(\mu,\nu)\,.
\]
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:uld-midpoint-one-step}
There is a universal constant \(C>0\) such that, for
\(0<h\leqslant h_0\),
\[
    W_{2,M}
       (\bm\pi K_h^{\mathrm{ExpMid}},\bm\pi)
    \leqslant C\sqrt d\,h^3\,.
\]
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:heun-oscillatory-lower}
There are universal \(c,h_0>0\) such that, for every \(d\geqslant2\)
and \(0<h\leqslant h_0\), the Heun sampler on
\eqref{eq:overdamped-common-target} has a unique invariant distribution
\(\widehat\pi_h^{\mathrm{Heun}}\in\mathcal P_2\), and
\begin{align}
 &W_2(\pi_hK_h^{\mathrm{Heun}},\pi_h)\geqslant c\sqrt d\,h^{3/2}\,, \qquad
       &&W_2(\widehat\pi_h^{\mathrm{Heun}},\pi_h)
 \geqslant c\sqrt d\,h^{3/2}\,,
 \label{eq:overdamped-common-ET}\\
 &\operatorname{KL}(\pi_hK_h^{\mathrm{Heun}}\mathrel{\Vert}\pi_h)\geqslant cdh^2\,,\qquad
       &&\operatorname{KL}(\widehat\pi_h^{\mathrm{Heun}}\mathrel{\Vert}\pi_h)
 \geqslant cdh^2\,.\notag
\end{align}
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:et-linear-kappa-lower}
There are universal \(c,C,\varepsilon_0>0\), \(d_0\geqslant4\), and
\(\kappa_0>1\) with the following property. Let \(d\geqslant d_0\),
\(\kappa\geqslant\kappa_0\), \(0<\varepsilon\leqslant\varepsilon_0\), and
let Heun use a fixed step with \(0<\beta h\leqslant c\).
There is an \(\alpha\)-strongly convex, \(\beta\)-smooth potential
\(V_h\) on \(\mathbb{R}^d\), with \(\beta/\alpha=\kappa\), and an initial
 distribution \(\nu_h\) such that
\[
    \mathbb{E}_{X_0\sim\nu_h}\bigl[V_h(X_0)-V_h(x_\star)\bigr]\leqslant Cd\,,
    \qquad x_\star\coloneqq\operatorname*{arg\,min} V_h\,.
\]
Writing \(\pi_h(\mathrm d x)\propto\mathrm{e}^{-V_h(x)}\,\mathrm d x\), for every
integer \(N\geqslant0\),
\(\sqrt\alpha\,W_2(\nu_h(K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})^N,\pi_h)
\leqslant\varepsilon\) implies
\begin{equation}
    N\geqslant {c\kappa
       \min\Bigl\{\frac{d^{1/3}}{\varepsilon^{2/3}}\,,
   \frac{d^{1/2}}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}\Bigr\}\log \frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\,.
    \label{eq:linear-kappa-endpoint-rate}
\end{equation}
In particular, in the moderate accuracy range
\(d^{-1}\leqslant\varepsilon\leqslant\varepsilon_0\),
\begin{equation}
    N\geqslant \frac{c\kappa d^{1/3}}{\varepsilon^{2/3}}
                 \log\frac1\varepsilon\,.
    \label{eq:linear-kappa-third-root}
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:exponential-midpoint-Gaussian-lower}
There are universal constants \(c>0\) and \(h_0\in(0,1]\) such that,
for the isotropic target \(V(x)=\lVert x\rVert^2/2\), with
\(\alpha=\beta=1\) and \(\gamma=2\), for every \(d\geqslant1\) and
\(0<h\leqslant h_0\), the kernel \(K_h^{\mathrm{ExpMid}}\) has a unique
invariant distribution \(\bm\pi_h^{\mathrm{ExpMid}}\) with finite second
moment. Its position marginal is \(\mathsf{N}(0,s_hI_d)\), where
\begin{equation*}
    s_h=1-\frac{h^2}{24}+O(h^3)\,.
\end{equation*}
The remainder is universal. In particular,
\begin{equation}
    W_2\bigl((\bm\pi_h^{\mathrm{ExpMid}})_x,\pi\bigr)
       \geqslant c\sqrt d\,h^2\,.
    \label{eq:exponential-midpoint-lower}
\end{equation}


More generally, for the dynamics corresponding to~\eqref{eq:em-anisotropic-potential},
the kernel has a unique invariant distribution in
\(\mathcal P_2(\mathbb{R}^{2d})\) whenever
\(0<\sqrt\beta\,h\leqslant h_0\), and its position bias satisfies
\[
 \sqrt\alpha\,W_2\bigl((\bm\pi_h^{\mathrm{ExpMid}})_x,\pi\bigr)
 =\frac{(\sqrt\beta\,h)^2}{48}\,
       \sqrt{\frac{d-1}{\kappa}+\frac1{\kappa^2}}\,
       \{1+O(\sqrt\beta\,h)\}\,.
\]
All constants and remainders are uniform in \(d,\alpha,\beta\).
Initialize
\[
 X_0=\alpha^{-1/2}e_d\,,\qquad
 P_0\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I_d)\,,
\]
with momentum independent of future randomness, where \(e_d\) is the
final coordinate vector. This start satisfies
\(\mathbb{E}[V(X_0)-V(x_\star)]=1/2\). For a universal \(C>0\) and every
integer \(N\geqslant0\),
\begin{equation}
 \sqrt\alpha\,W_2\bigl(\operatorname{Law}(\widehat X_N),\pi\bigr)
 \geqslant\max\Bigl\{
       c\sqrt{\frac d\kappa}\,(\sqrt\beta\,h)^2,
       \mathrm{e}^{-CN\sqrt\beta\,h/\kappa}\Bigr\}\,.
 \label{eq:em-anisotropic-finite-time}
\end{equation}
Consequently, for \(0<\varepsilon\leqslant1/2\), achieving
\(\sqrt\alpha\,W_2(\operatorname{Law}(\widehat X_N),\pi)\leqslant\varepsilon\)
requires
\begin{equation}
 N\geqslant c\,\Bigl(
       \kappa+\frac{\kappa^{3/4}d^{1/4}}{\varepsilon^{1/2}}
       \Bigr)\log\frac1\varepsilon\,.
 \label{eq:em-anisotropic-iteration}
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:overdamped-common-construction}
There are universal \(c,h_0>0\) such that, for every \(d\geqslant1\) and
\(0<h\leqslant h_0\), RLMC and PLMC on \(V(x)=\lVert x\rVert^2/2\)
have unique invariant distributions in \(\mathcal P_2\). Their errors satisfy
\begin{align}
    W_2(\widehat\pi_h^{\mathrm{RLMC}},\mathsf{N}(0,I_d))
       &\geqslant c\min\{dh^3,\sqrt{dh^3}\}\,,
       \label{eq:overdamped-common-RLMC}\\
    W_2(\widehat\pi_h^{\mathrm{PLMC},k},\mathsf{N}(0,I_d))
       &\geqslant c\min\{dh^3,\sqrt{dh^3}\}\,,\qquad k\geqslant2\,,\notag
\\
    W_2(\widehat\pi_h^{\mathrm{PLMC},1},\mathsf{N}(0,I_d))
       &\geqslant c\sqrt d\,h\,.
       \label{eq:overdamped-common-ULA}
\end{align}
The constants are uniform in \(k\). For \(k=1\), PLMC is the
Euler--Maruyama scheme (ULA).
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{prop:randomized-underdamped-Gaussian-lower}
There are universal \(c,h_0>0\) such that, for every \(d\geqslant1\) and
\(0<h\leqslant h_0\), RULMC and PULMC on \(V(x)=\lVert x\rVert^2/2\)
have unique invariant distributions in \(\mathcal P_2\). Their errors satisfy
\begin{align}
    W_2\bigl((\bm\pi_h^{\mathrm{RULMC}})_x,\mathsf{N}(0,I_d)\bigr)
       &\geqslant c\min\{dh^3,\sqrt{dh^3}\}\,,
       &&\notag\\
    W_2\bigl((\bm\pi_h^{\mathrm{PULMC},k})_x,\mathsf{N}(0,I_d)\bigr)
       &\geqslant c\min\{dh^3,\sqrt{dh^3}\}\,,
       &&k\geqslant2\,,\notag\\
    W_2\bigl((\bm\pi_h^{\mathrm{PULMC},1})_x,\mathsf{N}(0,I_d)\bigr)
       &\geqslant c\sqrt d\,h\,.\notag
\end{align}
The constants are uniform in \(k\). For \(k=1\), PULMC is the
exponential Euler scheme (ULMC).
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:midpoint-iteration-lower}
There are universal \(c,C,\varepsilon_0,h_0>0\) such that, for every
\(d\geqslant2\), \(0<h\leqslant h_0\), each method listed below,
and every Poisson grid size \(k\geqslant1\), there is an initial position distribution \(\nu_0\) satisfying
\[
 \mathbb{E}_{X_0\sim\nu_0}[V(X_0)-V(x_\star)]\leqslant Cd\,,
 \qquad \alphaW_2^2(\nu_0,\pi)\leqslant Cd\,,
 \qquad x_\star\coloneqq\operatorname*{arg\,min} V\,,
\]
with the following property. Write \(\nu_N\) for the position distribution
after \(N\) steps. For every \(0<\varepsilon\leqslant\varepsilon_0\)
and integer \(N\geqslant0\), position accuracy
\(\sqrt\alpha\,W_2(\nu_N,\pi)\leqslant\varepsilon\) requires:
\[
\begin{array}{l|c}
 \text{Method}&\text{Necessary iterations}\\ \hline
 \text{Heun}&N\geqslant cd^{1/3}\varepsilon^{-2/3}\\
 \text{Exponential midpoint}&N\geqslant cd^{1/4}\varepsilon^{-1/2}\\
 \text{RLMC or RULMC}
    &N\geqslant cd^{1/3}\varepsilon^{-1/3}\\
 \text{PLMC or PULMC with }k\geqslant2
    &N\geqslant cd^{1/3}\varepsilon^{-1/3}\\
 \text{ULA or ULMC (}k=1\text{)}
 &N\geqslant cd^{1/2}\varepsilon^{-1}
\end{array}
\]
Here, \(V\coloneqq V_{h,d}\) from \eqref{eq:overdamped-common-target} for Heun,
and \(V\coloneqq\lVert \cdot\rVert^2/2\) for every other method; the condition numbers
are bounded by a universal constant. The randomized methods use
quadrature choices shared across coordinates, and the constants are
uniform in the Poisson grid size \(k\).

For all underdamped methods, \(\gamma=2\) and
\(P_0\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I_d)\) is independent of \(X_0\) and future
randomness, and the initial positions are
\[
 \nu_0\coloneqq\delta_{e_d}\quad\text{for exponential midpoint}\,,\qquad
 \nu_0\coloneqq\mathsf{N}(e_d,I_d)\quad\text{for RULMC and PULMC}\,.
\]
These choices are independent of \(h,k\); the latter has
\(W_2(\nu_0,\pi)=1\). The overdamped initial distributions may depend
on the method, \(h\), and \(k\), but not on \(\varepsilon\) or \(N\).

Under the hypotheses of \Cref{thm:et-linear-kappa-lower}, its Heun target
and initialization satisfy the same two initialization bounds, and
\(\sqrt\alpha\,W_2(\nu_N,\pi)\leqslant\varepsilon\) requires
\[
 N\geqslant c\kappa
 \min\{d^{1/3}\varepsilon^{-2/3},
                  d^{1/2}\varepsilon^{-1/2}\}\log\frac1\varepsilon\,.
\]
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:et-displacement}
There is a universal constant \(C>0\) such that, for
\(0<t\leqslant h_0\) and \(x\in\mathbb{R}^d\),
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{E}\lVert X_t^x-x\rVert^2
    \leqslant C\,\bigl\{dt+t^2\,\lVert \nabla V(x)\rVert^2\bigr\}\,;
    \label{eq:et-displacement-pointwise}
\end{equation}
if \(X_0\sim\pi\), then
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{E}\lVert X_t-X_0\rVert^2\leqslant Cdt\,.
    \label{eq:et-displacement-stationary}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
The pointwise estimate is \cite[Lemma~4.1.8]{ChewiBook} with deterministic
initial state, \(\beta=1\), and \(t\leqslant h_0\leqslant1/3\).
For a stationary initial state, apply the same lemma and use
\(\int\lVert \nabla V\rVert^2\,\mathrm d \pi
=\int\Delta V\,\mathrm d \pi\leqslant d\).
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Couple the steps from \(x\) and \(y\) with the same Brownian increment.
Let \(X_h^{\mathrm{pred}}\) and \(Y_h^{\mathrm{pred}}\) be their
predictors.  The fundamental theorem of calculus gives
\[
\begin{split}
    H_{\mathrm{init}}&\coloneqq\int_0^1\nabla^2V\bigl(y+s\,(x-y)\bigr)\,\mathrm d s\,,\\
    H_{\mathrm{pred}}&\coloneqq\int_0^1\nabla^2V\bigl(Y_h^{\mathrm{pred}}
       +s\,(X_h^{\mathrm{pred}}-Y_h^{\mathrm{pred}})\bigr)\,\mathrm d s\,,
\end{split}
\]
so \(\nabla V(x)-\nabla V(y)=H_{\mathrm{init}}\,(x-y)\) and
\(\nabla V(X_h^{\mathrm{pred}})-\nabla V(Y_h^{\mathrm{pred}})
=H_{\mathrm{pred}}\,(X_h^{\mathrm{pred}}-Y_h^{\mathrm{pred}})\).
The predictor difference is \((I_d-hH_{\mathrm{init}})(x-y)\).  Substitution into
the update \eqref{eq:et-step} therefore yields
\begin{equation}
\begin{split}
    X^+-Y^+
    &=\bigl\{I_d-\tfrac h2\,H_{\mathrm{init}}-\tfrac h2\,H_{\mathrm{pred}}\,(I_d-hH_{\mathrm{init}})\bigr\}(x-y)\\
    &=\tfrac12\,\bigl\{I_d+(I_d-hH_{\mathrm{pred}})(I_d-hH_{\mathrm{init}})\bigr\}(x-y)\,.
    \label{eq:et-contraction-factorization}
\end{split}
\end{equation}
Both \(H_{\mathrm{init}}\) and \(H_{\mathrm{pred}}\) are symmetric with eigenvalues in
\([\kappa^{-1},1]\).  If \(0<h\leqslant1\), the eigenvalues of
\(I_d-hH_{\mathrm{init}}\) and \(I_d-hH_{\mathrm{pred}}\) lie in
\([0,1-h/\kappa]\); their operator norms are therefore at most
\(1-h/\kappa\).  No commutation of \(H_{\mathrm{init}}\) and \(H_{\mathrm{pred}}\) is needed:
submultiplicativity and \eqref{eq:et-contraction-factorization} give
\[
    \lVert X^+-Y^+\rVert
    \leqslant\frac{1+(1-h/\kappa)^2}{2}\,\lVert x-y\rVert
    \leqslant\Bigl(1-\frac h{2\kappa}\Bigr)\,\lVert x-y\rVert\,.
\]
Integrating this pathwise bound over an optimal coupling of the initial
states proves \eqref{eq:et-contraction-factor}.  Lipschitzness of the
force ensures that the kernel preserves \(\mathcal P_2(\mathbb{R}^d)\), so
Banach's fixed point theorem on the complete metric space
\((\mathcal P_2(\mathbb{R}^d),W_2)\) gives the invariant distribution.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Let \(0<h\leqslant1\).  For \(j\geqslant1\), contractivity gives
\[
    W_2\bigl(\nu(K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})^{j+1},
       \nu(K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})^j\bigr)
    \leqslant\rho^{j-1}
    W_2\bigl(\nu(K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})^2,\nu K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}}\bigr)\,.
\]
The triangle inequality from \(\nu\) to \(\nu(K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})^N\)
and summing as a geometric series suffice to bound
\(W_2(\nu,\nu(K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})^N)\) by the right-hand side of
\eqref{eq:et-local-framework}, uniformly in \(N\).  Since
\(W_2(\nu(K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})^N,\widehat\pi_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})
\leqslant\rho^NW_2(\nu,\widehat\pi_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})\to0\),
the claim follows.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:et-defect-second-moment}
There is a universal constant \(C>0\) such that, for
\(0<h\leqslant h_0\),
\[
    \mathbb{E}\lVert \mathcal E_h^{\mathrm{corr}}\rVert^2
    \leqslant Cdh^3\,.
\]
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Stationarity and \eqref{eq:et-corrector-L2} control the two corrector
terms in \eqref{eq:et-corrected-displacement}.  The quadrature term
satisfies, by \(1\)-Lipschitzness and
\eqref{eq:et-displacement-stationary},
\[
    \lVert \mathcal E_h\rVert_{L^2}
    \leqslant\int_0^h\lVert X_t-X_0\rVert_{L^2}\,\mathrm d t
       +\frac h2\,\lVert X_h-X_0\rVert_{L^2}
    \leqslant C\sqrt{dh^3}\,.
\]
Synchronous contraction of the exact diffusion
\cite[Lemma~5.1.6(1)]{ChewiBook} gives
\begin{equation}
    \lVert \overline X_h-X_h\rVert
    \leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-h/\kappa}\,\lVert \chi(X_0)\rVert\,.
    \label{eq:et-synchronous-contraction}
\end{equation}
Consequently, each force increment on the second line of
\eqref{eq:et-corrected-displacement} is bounded by
\(\lVert \chi(X_0)\rVert\).  The triangle inequality in \(L^2\) now gives
\(\lVert \mathcal E_h^{\mathrm{corr}}\rVert_{L^2}\leqslant
(2+h)\,\lVert \chi\rVert_{L^2(\pi)}+C\sqrt{dh^3}\), proving the claim.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:et-mean-bound}
For every \(h>0\),
\[
    \lVert m\rVert_{L^2(\pi)}
    \leqslant h\,\lVert \chi\rVert_{L^2(\pi)}\,.
\]
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
Reversibility and the discrete Poisson equation give
\[
    \mathbb{E}[\chi(X_0)+\mathcal E_h-\chi(X_h)\mid X_h]
    =P_h\chi(X_h)+e_h(X_h)-\chi(X_h)=0\,.
\]
Thus \eqref{eq:et-corrected-displacement} implies
\[
    m(X_h)=-\frac h2\,\mathbb{E}\bigl[
       \nabla V(X_0+\chi(X_0))-\nabla V(X_0)
       +\nabla V(\overline X_h)-\nabla V(X_h)\mid X_h\bigr]\,.
\]
Both increments are bounded by \(\lVert \chi(X_0)\rVert\), by
\(1\)-Lipschitzness and \eqref{eq:et-synchronous-contraction}.
Conditional Jensen's inequality proves the result.
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:et-malliavin-stein}
Let \(R\in L^2(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^d)\) be a centered, Malliavin-differentiable
random vector over an isonormal Gaussian probability space with
Cameron--Martin space \(\mathcal H\), and suppose that
\(D_GR\in L^4(\Omega;\mathfrak{S}_4)\).  Then the law of \(R\) has a
Stein kernel \(\tau_R\) satisfying
\[
    \mathbb{E}\lVert \tau_R\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
    \leqslant \lVert D_GR\rVert_{L^4(\Omega;\mathfrak{S}_4)}^4\,,
\]
where \(D_GR:\mathcal H\to\mathbb{R}^d\) denotes the Malliavin derivative of
\(R\).
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
The representation in \cite[Proposition~3.7]{NourdinPeccatiSwan2014},
transposed to match \eqref{eq:et-stein-identity}, gives
\[
    \tau_R(R)=\mathbb{E}\bigl[(D_GR)
       (-D_G\mathcal L_{\mathrm{OU}}^{-1}R)^*\mid R\bigr]\,.
\]
Here, \(\mathcal L_{\mathrm{OU}}\) is the Gaussian
Ornstein--Uhlenbeck generator and \( (\mathcal Q_t)_{t\ge 0} \) its Mehler
semigroup.  The pseudoinverse and gradient commutation formulas give
\begin{equation}
    -D_G\mathcal L_{\mathrm{OU}}^{-1}R
    =\int_0^\infty\mathrm{e}^{-t}\mathcal Q_tD_GR\,\mathrm d t\,.
    \label{eq:et-ou-gradient-representation}
\end{equation}
Jensen's inequality in the Mehler representation makes
\(\mathcal Q_t\) a contraction on
\(L^4(\Omega;\mathfrak S_4)\).  Minkowski's inequality in
\eqref{eq:et-ou-gradient-representation} therefore gives
\[
    \lVert -D_G\mathcal L_{\mathrm{OU}}^{-1}R\rVert_{L^4(\Omega;\mathfrak S_4)}
    \leqslant\lVert D_GR\rVert_{L^4(\Omega;\mathfrak S_4)}\,.
\]
Conditional Jensen, the Schatten inequality
\(\lVert AB^*\rVert_{\mathfrak S_2}\leqslant
\lVert A\rVert_{\mathfrak S_4}\,\lVert B\rVert_{\mathfrak S_4}\), and
Cauchy--Schwarz imply
\begin{align*}
    \mathbb{E}\lVert \tau_R\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
    &\leqslant\mathbb{E}\bigl[
       \lVert D_GR\rVert_{\mathfrak S_4}^2\,
       \lVert -D_G\mathcal L_{\mathrm{OU}}^{-1}R\rVert_{\mathfrak S_4}^2\bigr]\leqslant\lVert D_GR\rVert_{L^4(\Omega;\mathfrak S_4)}^4\,. \qedhere
\end{align*}
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:et-S4-corrector}
There is a universal constant \(C>0\) such that, for every \(h>0\), the
corrector in \eqref{eq:et-corrector} satisfies
\[
    \lVert \nabla\chi\rVert_{L^4(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_4)}
    \leqslant Cd^{1/4} h\,.
\]
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
\label{prop:et-S4-derivative}
There is a universal constant \(C>0\) such that, for
\(0<h\leqslant h_0\), the fluctuation \(R\) in
\eqref{eq:et-mean-fluctuation} satisfies
\begin{equation}
    \lVert D_GR\rVert_{L^4(\Omega;\mathfrak{S}_4)}
    \leqslant Cd^{1/4} h^{3/2}\,.
    \label{eq:et-DR-S4}
\end{equation}
The norm averages over both the endpoint \(X_h\sim\pi\) and the
Brownian realization with that endpoint fixed.
Consequently, it has conditional Stein kernels \(\tau_{X_h}\) such that
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{E}\lVert \tau_{X_h}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
    \leqslant Cdh^6\,.
    \label{eq:et-Stein-kernel-bound}
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-8 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Condition on \(X_h=y\).  By reversibility, the conditional law of
\((X_{h-t})_{0\leqslant t\leqslant h}\) is the law of a Langevin diffusion
started from \(y\), which we realize on canonical Wiener space.  Write
\(\mathcal H\coloneqq L^2([0,h];\mathbb{R}^d)\), let \(G\) be the associated isonormal
Gaussian process over \(\mathcal H\), and define
\[
    \mathcal J_t\coloneqq D_GX_{h-t}:\mathcal H\to\mathbb{R}^d\,,
    \qquad 0\leqslant t\leqslant h\,.
\]
Thus, \(\mathcal J_t\) is the Malliavin derivative of the reversed path at
time \(t\).  To identify it, fix \(f\in\mathcal H\) and shift
the canonical Brownian path in the Cameron--Martin direction
\(t\mapsto\int_0^t f_s\,\mathrm d s\).  Differentiating the resulting Langevin SDE
with respect to this shift shows that
\[
    \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d t}\,(\mathcal J_tf)
    =-\nabla^2V(X_{h-t})\mathcal J_tf+\sqrt2\,f_t\,,
    \qquad \mathcal J_0f=0\,.
\]
For \(0\leqslant s\leqslant t\leqslant h\), let \(J_{t,s}\) be the
homogeneous evolution operator, characterized by
\[
    \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d t}\,J_{t,s}=-\nabla^2V(X_{h-t})J_{t,s}\,,
    \qquad J_{s,s}=I\,.
\]
For fixed \(s\) and \(v\in\mathbb{R}^d\), convexity of \(V\) gives
\[
    \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d t}\,\lVert J_{t,s}v\rVert^2
    =-2\,\langle J_{t,s}v,\nabla^2V(X_{h-t})J_{t,s}v\rangle
    \leqslant0\,.
\]
Since \(J_{s,s}=I\), it follows that
\(\lVert J_{t,s}v\rVert\leqslant\lVert v\rVert\), and hence
\(\lVert J_{t,s}\rVert_{\mathfrak{S}_\infty}\leqslant1\).  For every
\(f\in\mathcal H\), variation of constants now yields
\[
    \mathcal J_tf=\sqrt2\int_0^tJ_{t,s}f_s\,\mathrm d s\,.
\]
Cauchy--Schwarz and the bound on \(J_{t,s}\) give
\(\lVert \mathcal J_t\rVert_{\mathfrak{S}_\infty}\leqslant\sqrt{2t}\).  The
integral kernel representation gives
\[
    \lVert \mathcal J_t\rVert_{\mathfrak{S}_2}^2
    =2\int_0^t\lVert J_{t,s}\rVert_{\mathfrak{S}_2}^2\,\mathrm d s
    \leqslant2dt\,.
\]
In summary,
\begin{equation}
    \lVert \mathcal J_t\rVert_{\mathfrak{S}_\infty}\leqslant\sqrt{2t}\,,
    \qquad
    \lVert \mathcal J_t\rVert_{\mathfrak{S}_2}\leqslant\sqrt{2dt}\,.
    \label{eq:et-K-op-HS}
\end{equation}
H\"older's inequality for the singular values therefore gives
\begin{equation}
    \lVert \mathcal J_t\rVert_{\mathfrak{S}_4}
    \leqslant\lVert \mathcal J_t\rVert_{\mathfrak{S}_\infty}^{1/2}\,
       \lVert \mathcal J_t\rVert_{\mathfrak{S}_2}^{1/2}
    \leqslant\sqrt{2t}\,d^{1/4}\,.
    \label{eq:et-K-S4}
\end{equation}

Because \(X_h=y\) is held fixed when differentiating with respect to the
driving Brownian path,
\(D_G\{m(X_h)\}=D_G\{\chi(X_h)\}=0\) as they depend only on this endpoint.  Differentiating
\eqref{eq:et-corrected-displacement} gives
\[
\begin{split}
    D_GR={}&D_G\{\chi(X_0)\}+D_G\mathcal E_h \\
           &\qquad{} -\frac h2\,D_G\{\nabla V(X_0+\chi(X_0))-\nabla V(X_0)\}
      -\frac h2\,D_G\{\nabla V(\overline X_h)-\nabla V(X_h)\}\,.
\end{split}
\]
We estimate these four terms in turn.  The Malliavin chain rule first gives
\[
    D_G\{\chi(X_0)\}
    =\nabla\chi(X_0)^\top D_GX_0
    =\nabla\chi(X_0)^\top\mathcal J_h\,.
\]
Using \eqref{eq:et-K-op-HS}, then averaging over the reverse noise
and the stationary endpoint, gives
\begin{equation}
    \lVert D_G\{\chi(X_0)\}\rVert_{L^4(\Omega;\mathfrak S_4)}
    \leqslant\sqrt{2h}\,\lVert \nabla\chi\rVert_{L^4(\pi;\mathfrak S_4)}
    \leqslant Cd^{1/4} h^{3/2}\,,
    \label{eq:et-u-conditional-derivative}
\end{equation}
where the last inequality is \Cref{lem:et-S4-corrector}.

We next bound \(D_G\mathcal E_h\).  Applying the Malliavin chain rule to the
formula for \(\mathcal E_h=\widetilde X_h-X_h\) in
\eqref{eq:et-quadrature-error} gives
\[
\begin{split}
    D_G\mathcal E_h
    ={}&\int_0^h\nabla^2V(X_t)\mathcal J_{h-t}\,\mathrm d t
       -\frac h2\,\nabla^2V(X_0)\mathcal J_h\,.
\end{split}
\]
This uses \(\mathcal J_0=D_GX_h=0\) because the endpoint \(X_h=y\) is held
fixed.  Using
\(\lVert \nabla^2V\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant1\), Minkowski's inequality,
and \eqref{eq:et-K-S4}, we now obtain
\[
\begin{split}
    \lVert D_G\mathcal E_h\rVert_{L^4(\Omega;\mathfrak{S}_4)}
    &\leqslant \int_0^h\lVert \mathcal J_{h-t}\rVert_{\mathfrak{S}_4}\,\mathrm d t
       +\frac h2\,\lVert \mathcal J_h\rVert_{\mathfrak{S}_4}\leqslant\sqrt2\,d^{1/4}\,
       \Bigl\{\int_0^h\sqrt{h-t}\,\mathrm d t+\frac h2\,\sqrt h\Bigr\}\leqslant Cd^{1/4} h^{3/2}\,.
\end{split}
\]

For the term
\(D_G\{\nabla V(X_0+\chi(X_0))-\nabla V(X_0)\}\), the chain rule gives
\[
\begin{split}
    &D_G\{\nabla V(X_0+\chi(X_0))-\nabla V(X_0)\}=
       \bigl\{\nabla^2V(X_0+\chi(X_0))
          (I+\nabla\chi(X_0)^\top)-\nabla^2V(X_0)\bigr\}\mathcal J_h\,.
\end{split}
\]
Using \(\lVert \nabla^2V\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant1\),
\Cref{lem:et-S4-corrector}, \eqref{eq:et-K-op-HS}, and
\eqref{eq:et-K-S4}, we obtain
\[
\begin{split}
    &\frac h2\,\lVert D_G\{\nabla V(X_0+\chi(X_0))-\nabla V(X_0)\}\rVert_{L^4(\Omega;\mathfrak{S}_4)}\leqslant
    Cd^{1/4} h^{3/2}\,.
\end{split}
\]

It remains to bound
\(D_G\{\nabla V(\overline X_h)-\nabla V(X_h)\}\).  Return to the
forward time coordinate, let \(\overline X_t\) be the diffusion started from
\(X_0+\chi(X_0)\) with the same Brownian path, and put
\(\eta_t\coloneqq\overline X_t-X_t\).  Then \(D_GX_t=\mathcal J_{h-t}\) and adding and subtracting $\nabla^2 V(\overline X_t) \mathcal J_{h-t}$ gives
\[
\begin{split}
    \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d t}\,D_G\eta_t
    ={}&-\nabla^2V(\overline X_t)D_G\eta_t-\{\nabla^2V(\overline X_t)-\nabla^2V(X_t)\}\mathcal J_{h-t}\,,
    \qquad
    D_G\eta_0=\nabla\chi(X_0)^\top\mathcal J_h\,.
\end{split}
\]
The fundamental solution of the homogeneous equation is a contraction.
Since
\(\lVert \nabla^2V(\overline X_t)-\nabla^2V(X_t)\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
\leqslant2\),
variation of constants, \Cref{lem:et-S4-corrector},
\eqref{eq:et-K-op-HS}, and \eqref{eq:et-K-S4} bound the initial
variation by \(Cd^{1/4} h^{3/2}\) and the forcing at time \(t\) by
\(Cd^{1/4} \sqrt{h-t}\). Integrating the latter gives
\[
\begin{split}
    \lVert D_G\eta_h\rVert_{L^4(\Omega;\mathfrak{S}_4)}
    &\leqslant C\sqrt h\,\lVert \nabla\chi\rVert_{L^4(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_4)}
    +2\int_0^h\lVert \mathcal J_{h-t}\rVert_{\mathfrak{S}_4}\,\mathrm d t\leqslant Cd^{1/4} h^{3/2}\,.
\end{split}
\]
Because \(X_h\) is fixed under the conditioning,
\(D_G\overline X_h=D_G\eta_h\).  Thus,
\begin{equation}
\begin{split}
    &\frac h2\,\lVert D_G\{\nabla V(\overline X_h)-\nabla V(X_h)\}\rVert_{L^4(\Omega;\mathfrak{S}_4)}\leqslant C d^{1/4} h^{5/2}\,.
\end{split}
\label{eq:et-terminal-force-S4-derivative}
\end{equation}
Combining \eqref{eq:et-u-conditional-derivative}--\eqref{eq:et-terminal-force-S4-derivative} and using \(h\leqslant h_0\leqslant1\) proves
\eqref{eq:et-DR-S4}.

For $\pi$-almost every endpoint \(y\), \Cref{lem:et-malliavin-stein} gives a Stein kernel
\(\tau_y\) satisfying
\[
    \mathbb{E}\bigl[\lVert \tau_y\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\mid X_h=y\bigr]
    \leqslant
    \mathbb{E}\bigl[\lVert D_GR\rVert_{\mathfrak{S}_4}^4
       \mid X_h=y\bigr]\,.
\]
Since \(X_h\sim\pi\), integrating this inequality over the endpoint and
using the tower property gives
\[
\begin{split}
    \mathbb{E}\lVert \tau_{X_h}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
    &\leqslant
       \mathbb{E}\lVert D_GR\rVert_{\mathfrak{S}_4}^4=\lVert D_GR\rVert_{L^4(\Omega;\mathfrak{S}_4)}^4\leqslant
       \bigl(Cd^{1/4} h^{3/2}\bigr)^4
     =C^4dh^6\,,
\end{split}
\]
where the inequality is \eqref{eq:et-DR-S4}.  Renaming the universal
constant proves \eqref{eq:et-Stein-kernel-bound}.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:et-witten-endpoints}
For every \(t\geqslant0\) and \(2\leqslant p\leqslant\infty\),
\begin{equation}
    \lVert P_t^{(1)}M\rVert_{L^p(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_p)}
    \leqslant \mathrm{e}^{-t/\kappa}\,\lVert M\rVert_{L^p(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_p)}\,.
    \label{eq:et-witten-real-contraction}
\end{equation}
On \(L^2(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_2)\), \(-L^{(1)}\) is positive self-adjoint and
\begin{equation}
\begin{split}
    \langle M,-L^{(1)}M\rangle_{L^2(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_2)}
    ={}&\int\lVert \nabla M\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\,\mathrm d \pi+\int\sum_{j\in[d]}
          \langle M_j,\nabla^2V\,M_j\rangle\,\mathrm d \pi\,.
\end{split}
\label{eq:et-witten-quadratic-form}
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
    \lVert \mathrm{e}^{zL^{(1)}}M\rVert_{L^2(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_2)}
    \leqslant\lVert M\rVert_{L^2(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_2)}
    \qquad\text{whenever }\operatorname{Re}z\geqslant0\,.
    \label{eq:et-witten-L2-complex}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
The Feynman--Kac formula and the ideal property of Schatten norms give
\[
    \lVert P_t^{(1)}M(x)\rVert_{\mathfrak S_p}
    \leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-t/\kappa}\,\mathbb{E}\lVert M(X_t^x)\rVert_{\mathfrak S_p}\,.
\]
Jensen's inequality and stationarity give
\eqref{eq:et-witten-real-contraction} for finite \(p\); the essential
supremum gives the case \(p=\infty\).  Integration by parts proves
\eqref{eq:et-witten-quadratic-form} on smooth compactly supported
fields.  Closing this symmetric form defines the non-negative
self-adjoint operator \(-L^{(1)}\), and the spectral theorem proves
\eqref{eq:et-witten-L2-complex}.
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:et-witten-analyticity}
The semigroup \(\mathrm{e}^{zL^{(1)}}\) extends to a contractive analytic semigroup on
\(L^4(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_4)\) in the sector \(\lvert \arg z \rvert<\uppi/4\).  In
particular, \(-L^{(1)}\) is sectorial on \(L^4(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_4)\):
its spectrum is contained in the closed sector
\(\{\lvert\arg z\rvert\leqslant\uppi/4\}\), and for each fixed
\(\vartheta\in(\uppi/4,\uppi/2)\) there is a constant
\(C_\vartheta\) such that
\begin{equation}
    \lVert (z+L^{(1)})^{-1}\rVert_{L^4(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_4)\to L^4(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_4)}
    \leqslant\frac{C_\vartheta}{\lvert z\rvert}
    \qquad\text{whenever }\lvert\arg z\rvert\geqslant\vartheta\,.
    \label{eq:et-witten-resolvent}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
The interpolation identity for Bochner--Schatten spaces
(\Cref{subsec:tb-interpolation}) gives
\begin{equation}
    [L^\infty(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_\infty),L^2(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_2)]_{1/2}
    =L^4(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_4)\,,
    \label{eq:et-matrix-interpolation}
\end{equation}
isometrically~\cite{BerghLofstrom1976}.

Fix \(\lvert\theta\rvert<\uppi/4\) and, for \(t>0\), consider on the strip
\(0\leqslant\operatorname{Re}\zeta\leqslant1\) the analytic family
\[
\mathcal F(\zeta)\coloneqq\exp(t\mathrm{e}^{2\boldsymbol i\theta\zeta}L^{(1)})\,.
\]
If \(\zeta=\boldsymbol iy\), then \(t\mathrm{e}^{2\boldsymbol i\theta\zeta}=t\mathrm{e}^{-2\theta y}>0\), so
\Cref{lem:et-witten-endpoints} shows that \(\mathcal F(\boldsymbol iy)\) is a
contraction on \(L^\infty(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_\infty)\).
If \(\zeta=1+\boldsymbol iy\), then
\(t\mathrm{e}^{-2\theta y}\mathrm{e}^{2\boldsymbol i\theta}\) has positive real part, so
\eqref{eq:et-witten-L2-complex} shows that
\(\mathcal F(1+\boldsymbol iy)\) is a contraction on
\(L^2(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_2)\).
Stein's interpolation theorem~\cite{Stein1956} at \(\zeta=1/2\) and
\eqref{eq:et-matrix-interpolation} yield
\[
    \lVert \exp(t\mathrm{e}^{\boldsymbol i\theta}L^{(1)})\rVert_{L^4(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_4)\to L^4(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_4)}
    \leqslant1\,.
\]
The argument is first applied on the dense compatible core
\(L^\infty(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_\infty)\cap L^2(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_2)\) and then
extended to \(L^4(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_4)\).  Strong continuity on
\(L^4(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_4)\) follows from the same density argument.
This proves the first assertion.

The resolvent of \(-L^{(1)}\) is the Laplace transform of the
semigroup, and analyticity lets the integration ray be rotated by any
angle below \(\uppi/4\), as in \eqref{eq:tb-rotated-laplace}.  Choosing
the rotation as in \Cref{subsec:tb-interpolation} gives
\eqref{eq:et-witten-resolvent} with
\(C_\vartheta=1/\sin((\vartheta-\uppi/4)/2)\).
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:et-S4-multiplier}
There is a universal constant \(C>0\) such that for every $h > 0$,
\begin{equation}
    \lVert r(-hL^{(1)})\rVert_{L^4(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_4)\to L^4(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_4)}
    \leqslant C\,.
    \label{eq:et-S4-multiplier}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
We apply \Cref{lem:tb-sectorial-multiplier} on
\(\mathcal X=L^4(\pi;\mathfrak S_4)\) with \(\mathcal T=-L^{(1)}\) and
\(f=r\), which also defines \(r(-hL^{(1)})\) on this space.  By
\Cref{lem:et-witten-analyticity}, \(-L^{(1)}\) is sectorial of angle
\(\uppi/4\), with constants depending only on the angle.
Fix \(\vartheta\coloneqq3\uppi/8\). It remains to verify the hypotheses
on \(r\) in the sector
\(\Sigma_\vartheta\coloneqq\{z\in\mathbb C\setminus\{0\}:\lvert\arg z\rvert<\vartheta\}\),
recalled in \Cref{subsec:tb-interpolation}, with \(r(0)=0\), \(r(\infty)=-1/2\), and
\(s=1\).

\emph{Holomorphy.}  The poles of \(\coth(z/2)\) are at
\(2\uppi\boldsymbol ik\), \(k\in\mathbb Z\).  The pole at zero cancels
with \(z^{-1}\), since \(\tfrac12\coth(z/2)=z^{-1}+z/12+O(z^3)\); the
other poles lie on the imaginary axis, outside \(\Sigma_\vartheta\).
Thus \(r\) is holomorphic in \(\Sigma_\vartheta\) and in the disc
\(\{\lvert z\rvert<2\uppi\}\).

\emph{Behavior at zero.}  Since \(r\) is holomorphic in
\(\{\lvert z\rvert<2\uppi\}\) with \(r(0)=0\), there is a universal
constant \(C\) such that \(\lvert r(z)\rvert\leqslant C\,\lvert z\rvert\)
for \(\lvert z\rvert\leqslant1\).

\emph{Behavior at infinity.}  The identity
\(\coth(z/2)=1+2/(\mathrm{e}^z-1)\) gives
\[
    r(z)+\frac12=\frac1z-\frac1{\mathrm{e}^z-1}\,.
\]
For \(z\in\Sigma_\vartheta\), we have
\(\operatorname{Re}z\geqslant\lvert z\rvert\cos\vartheta\), so
\(\lvert\mathrm{e}^z-1\rvert\geqslant\mathrm{e}^{\operatorname{Re}z}-1
\geqslant\lvert z\rvert\cos\vartheta\).  Hence
\(\lvert r(z)+1/2\rvert\leqslant(1+1/\cos\vartheta)/\lvert z\rvert\).

\Cref{lem:tb-sectorial-multiplier} now bounds
\(\lVert r(-hL^{(1)})\rVert_{L^4(\pi;\mathfrak S_4)\to L^4(\pi;\mathfrak S_4)}\)
uniformly in \(h>0\) by a universal constant, which proves
\eqref{eq:et-S4-multiplier}.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Gradient intertwining gives \(\nabla P_t=P_t^{(1)}\nabla\).  Differentiate
the finite partial sums in \eqref{eq:et-corrector-series} and use
\eqref{eq:et-witten-real-contraction} to pass to the limit in
\(L^4(\pi;\mathfrak{S}_4)\).  Since $\nabla\chi=h\,r(-hL^{(1)})\,\nabla^2V$,
we can apply \Cref{lem:et-S4-multiplier} and the pointwise bound
\(\lVert \nabla^2V\rVert_{\mathfrak{S}_4}\leqslant d^{1/4}\).
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:et-gaussian-convolution}
Let \(R\in L^2(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^d)\) be centered, suppose that its law
admits a square-integrable Stein kernel \(\tau_R\), and let
\(\xi\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\sigma^2I_d)\) be independent of \(R\).  Then,
\[
    W_2^2\bigl(\operatorname{Law}(R+\xi),\operatorname{Law}(\xi)\bigr)
    \leqslant{\frac1{4\sigma^2}}\,\mathbb{E}\lVert \tau_R\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\,.
\]
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Set \(\mathsf X_\theta\coloneqq\xi+\theta R\), for
\(0\leqslant\theta\leqslant1\).
For \(\varphi\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb{R}^d)\), the Stein identity
\eqref{eq:et-stein-identity}, followed by Gaussian integration by parts,
gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
    \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d\theta}\,\mathbb{E}\varphi(\mathsf X_\theta)
    &=\mathbb{E}\langle R,\nabla\varphi(\mathsf X_\theta)\rangle
      =\theta\,\mathbb{E}\operatorname{tr}\{\tau_R\nabla^2\varphi(\mathsf X_\theta)\}\\
    &=\frac\theta{\sigma^2}\,\mathbb{E}\langle \tau_R\,\xi,\nabla\varphi(\mathsf X_\theta)\rangle\,.
\end{aligned}
\]
Here the first integration by parts holds with \(\xi\) fixed, and the
second with \(R\) fixed.  Thus
\(v_\theta(\mathsf X_\theta)\coloneqq\frac\theta{\sigma^2}\,\mathbb{E}[\tau_R\,\xi\mid\mathsf X_\theta]\)
is a velocity for the continuity equation of \(\operatorname{Law}(\mathsf X_\theta)\).
Conditional Jensen's inequality and independence imply
\[
    \lVert v_\theta\rVert_{L^2(\operatorname{Law}(\mathsf X_\theta))}^2
    \leqslant\frac{\theta^2}{\sigma^4}\,\mathbb{E}\lVert \tau_R\,\xi\rVert^2
    =\frac{\theta^2}{\sigma^2}\,\mathbb{E}\lVert \tau_R\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\,.
\]
The metric speed bound
\cite[Theorem~8.3.1]{AmbrosioGigliSavare2008} therefore yields
\[
    W_2\bigl(\operatorname{Law}(R+\xi),\operatorname{Law}(\xi)\bigr)
    \leqslant\int_0^1\lVert v_\theta\rVert_{L^2(\operatorname{Law}(\mathsf X_\theta))}\,\mathrm d\theta
    \leqslant\frac1{2\sigma}\,
       \bigl(\mathbb{E}\lVert \tau_R\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\bigr)^{1/2}\,.\qedhere
\]
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
Theorem~E.4 and Remark~E.6(ii) of \cite{PaulinWhalley2026} give the
same squared Wasserstein bound with coefficient \( (\log2)/\sigma^2\),
by combining a relative entropy estimate with Gaussian Talagrand.
The direct transport proof gives \(1/(4\sigma^2)\) under the same
square-integrable Stein kernel assumption; their theorem additionally
controls relative entropy.  The numerical constant \(1/4\) is sharp:
in dimension one, for independent \(R\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\varepsilon)\) and
\(\xi\sim\mathsf{N}(0,\sigma^2)\), one may take \(\tau_R=\varepsilon\), and
\[
    W_2^2\bigl(\operatorname{Law}(R+\xi),\operatorname{Law}(\xi)\bigr)
    =\bigl(\sqrt{\sigma^2+\varepsilon}-\sigma\bigr)^2
    \sim\frac{\varepsilon^2}{4\sigma^2}\,,
    \qquad \varepsilon\downarrow0\,.
\]
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:et-Gaussian-convolution-transport}
There is a universal constant \(C>0\) with the following property.
Let \(0<h\leqslant h_0\) and \(z\in\mathbb{R}^d\), and let
\(R\in L^2(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^d)\) be centered and independent of the Brownian
motion driving the auxiliary step \eqref{eq:et-exact-reference}, with
a law admitting a square-integrable Stein kernel \(\tau_R\).  Then,
\begin{equation}
    W_2^2\bigl(\operatorname{Law}(\widetilde X_h^{z+R}),
       \operatorname{Law}(\widetilde X_h^{z})\bigr)
    \leqslant C\,\Bigl\{
       \frac{\mathbb{E}\lVert \tau_R\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2}{h}
       +h^2\,\mathbb{E}\lVert R\rVert^2
      \Bigr\}\,.
\label{eq:et-Gaussian-convolution-transport}
\end{equation}
The same estimate holds conditionally on a preceding \(\sigma\)-field
when \(z\) is measurable with respect to it and the Brownian motion of
the auxiliary step is fresh and independent of that field and \(R\).
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
Let the auxiliary step from \(z+R\) be driven by a Brownian motion
\(B\) independent of \(R\), and the step from \(z\) by a second
Brownian motion \(\widetilde B\); the joint law of
\((R,B,\widetilde B)\) is constructed as follows.  Take an optimal
\(W_2\) coupling of the endpoint variables \(R+\sqrt2B_h\) and
\(\sqrt2\widetilde B_h\); by \Cref{lem:et-gaussian-convolution},
applied with \(\sigma^2=2h\), its cost satisfies
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{E}\lVert \delta\rVert^2
    \leqslant{\frac1{8h}}\,\mathbb{E}\lVert \tau_R\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2\,,
    \qquad
    \delta\coloneqq R+\sqrt2B_h-\sqrt2\widetilde B_h\,.
    \label{eq:et-endpoint-coupling}
\end{equation}
First sample \((R,B_h)\) conditionally on the coupled value of
\(R+\sqrt2B_h\).  This gives a joint law of
\((R,B_h,\widetilde B_h)\) with the required marginals.
To construct the full paths, take a standard Brownian bridge
\((b_t)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant h}\), independent of this triple, and set
\[
    B_t\coloneqq\frac th\, B_h+b_t\,,\qquad
    \widetilde B_t\coloneqq\frac th\,\widetilde B_h+b_t\,,
    \qquad 0\leqslant t\leqslant h\,.
\]
Each path is marginally a Brownian motion.  Their common bridge cancels
in the difference, giving
\(B_t-\widetilde B_t=\frac th\,(B_h-\widetilde B_h)\).
Thus, their drift-free parts differ by
\[
    R+\sqrt2B_t-\sqrt2\widetilde B_t
    =\Bigl(1-\frac th\Bigr)\,R+\frac th\,\delta\,,
\]
whose norm never exceeds \(\lVert R\rVert+\lVert \delta\rVert\).  By
\(1\)-Lipschitzness of \(\nabla V\) and Gr\"{o}nwall's inequality, the
diffusions \(X_t^{z+R}\) and \(X_t^{z}\), driven by \(B\) and by
\(\widetilde B\) respectively, therefore satisfy, for
\(0\leqslant t\leqslant h\leqslant h_0\),
\[
    \lVert X_t^{z+R}-X_t^{z}\rVert
    \leqslant C\,\bigl(\lVert R\rVert+\lVert \delta\rVert\bigr)\,.
\]
The outputs of the two auxiliary steps differ by
\[
    \widetilde X_h^{z+R}-\widetilde X_h^{z}
    =\delta
    -\frac h2\,\bigl\{\nabla V(z+R)-\nabla V(z)\bigr\}
    -\frac h2\,\bigl\{\nabla V(X_h^{z+R})-\nabla V(X_h^{z})\bigr\}\,,
\]
and the two preceding bounds, with \(h\leqslant1\), give
\[
    \mathbb{E}\lVert \widetilde X_h^{z+R}-\widetilde X_h^{z}\rVert^2
    \leqslant C\,\bigl\{\mathbb{E}\lVert \delta\rVert^2+h^2\,\mathbb{E}\lVert R\rVert^2\bigr\}\,.
\]
Together with \eqref{eq:et-endpoint-coupling}, this proves
\eqref{eq:et-Gaussian-convolution-transport}.  Conditional
disintegration proves the final assertion.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- proposition-9: body_md -->
\label{prop:et-invariant-bias}
There is a universal constant \(C>0\) such that, for
\(0<h\leqslant h_0\),
\begin{equation}
    \kappa^{-1}W_2^2(\widehat\pi_h^{\mathrm{Heun}},\pi)
    \leqslant C\kappa dh^3\,.
    \label{eq:et-direct-invariant-bound}
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-9 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
Fix \(0<h\leqslant h_0\) and apply
\Cref{lem:et-local-framework} with the reference law
\(\nu=(I+\chi)_\#\pi\) from \eqref{eq:et-corrected-law}.  We verify the
two distances on the right-hand side of
\eqref{eq:et-local-framework}.

The coupling \eqref{eq:et-mean-fluctuation} and
\Cref{lem:et-defect-second-moment} immediately give
\begin{equation}
    W_2^2(\nu\widetilde K_h,\nu)\leqslant Cdh^3\,.
    \label{eq:et-corrected-one-step-cost}
\end{equation}
For the distance between consecutive iterates, apply a fresh auxiliary
step to the coupled starting points
\[
    X_h+\chi(X_h)\sim\nu\,,
    \qquad
    X_h+\chi(X_h)+m(X_h)+R\sim\nu\widetilde K_h\,.
\]
Condition on \(X_h\).  Removing the mean \(m(X_h)\) by synchronous
coupling costs at most \(C\,\lVert m\rVert_{L^2(\pi)}^2\leqslant Cdh^5\)
in squared distance, by \Cref{lem:et-mean-bound} and
\eqref{eq:et-corrector-L2}.  For the centered fluctuation \(R\),
\Cref{lem:et-Gaussian-convolution-transport} and
\eqref{eq:et-Stein-kernel-bound} give, after averaging over the endpoint,
\[
    C\,\bigl\{h^{-1}\,\mathbb{E}\lVert \tau_{X_h}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^2
       +h^2\,\mathbb{E}\lVert R\rVert^2\bigr\}\leqslant Cdh^5\,.
\]
Here \(\mathbb{E}\lVert R\rVert^2\leqslant\mathbb{E}\lVert \mathcal E_h^{\mathrm{corr}}\rVert^2\leqslant Cdh^3\)
by conditional centering and \Cref{lem:et-defect-second-moment}.
The triangle inequality therefore yields
\begin{equation}
    W_2^2(\nu\widetilde K_h^2,\nu\widetilde K_h)
    \leqslant Cdh^5\,.
    \label{eq:et-lag-one-cost}
\end{equation}

We next replace \(\widetilde K_h\) by the Heun kernel
\(K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}}\) defined by
\eqref{eq:et-predictor}--\eqref{eq:et-step}.  From a starting point \(x\),
drive the exact diffusion and the auxiliary step by the same Brownian path,
and use its endpoint \(B_h\) in the Heun step. Recall from
\eqref{eq:et-exact-reference} that
\(\widetilde X_h^x=x-\tfrac h2\,\{\nabla V(x)+\nabla V(X_h^x)\}+\sqrt2B_h\).
Thus
\[
    X^+-\widetilde X_h^x
    =-\frac h2\,\bigl\{\nabla V(X_h^{\mathrm{pred}})
       -\nabla V(X_h^x)\bigr\}\,.
\]
Moreover,
\[
    X_h^{\mathrm{pred}}-X_h^x
    =\int_0^h\bigl\{\nabla V(X_t^x)-\nabla V(x)\bigr\}\,\mathrm d t\,.
\]
Jensen's inequality, \(1\)-Lipschitzness of \(\nabla V\), and
\eqref{eq:et-displacement-pointwise} therefore give
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{E}\lVert X^+-\widetilde X_h^x\rVert^2
    \leqslant C\,\bigl\{dh^5+h^6\,\lVert \nabla V(x)\rVert^2\bigr\}\,.
    \label{eq:et-implementation-local}
\end{equation}
Since \(\nu=(I+\chi)_\#\pi\), integration by parts and
\eqref{eq:et-corrector-L2} imply
\[
\begin{split}
    \int\lVert \nabla V\rVert^2\,\mathrm d \nu
    &=\int\lVert \nabla V(x+\chi(x))\rVert^2\,\pi(\mathrm d x)\leqslant2\int\lVert \nabla V\rVert^2\,\mathrm d \pi
       +2\,\lVert \chi\rVert_{L^2(\pi)}^2
     \leqslant Cd\,.
\end{split}
\]
The definition of \(\widetilde X_h^x\) and
\eqref{eq:et-displacement-pointwise} then give
\[
    \int\mathbb{E}\lVert \widetilde X_h^x-x\rVert^2\,\nu(\mathrm d x)
    \leqslant C\,\Bigl\{dh+h^2\int\lVert \nabla V\rVert^2\,\mathrm d \nu\Bigr\}
    \leqslant Cdh\,.
\]
Using \(1\)-Lipschitzness once more,
\begin{align*}
    \int\lVert \nabla V\rVert^2\,\mathrm d(\nu\widetilde K_h)
    &\leqslant2\int\lVert \nabla V\rVert^2\,\mathrm d \nu
       +2\int\mathbb{E}\lVert \widetilde X_h^x-x\rVert^2\,\nu(\mathrm d x)
    \leqslant Cd\,.
\end{align*}
Integrating \eqref{eq:et-implementation-local} first against \(\nu\) and
then against \(\nu\widetilde K_h\) proves
\begin{equation}
\begin{split}
    W_2^2(\nu K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}},\nu\widetilde K_h)
    &\leqslant Cdh^5\,,\qquad
    W_2^2((\nu\widetilde K_h)K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}},
       (\nu\widetilde K_h)\widetilde K_h)
    \leqslant Cdh^5\,.
\end{split}
    \label{eq:et-kernel-comparison-costs}
\end{equation}

The triangle inequality and
\eqref{eq:et-corrected-one-step-cost} transfer the first cost to
\(K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}}\).  For the second cost, insert
\((\nu\widetilde K_h)K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}}\),
\(\nu\widetilde K_h^2\), and \(\nu\widetilde K_h\) between
\(\nu(K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})^2\) and \(\nu K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}}\).
Contractivity bounds the first inserted distance by
\(\rhoW_2(\nu K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}},\nu\widetilde K_h)\).
Thus \eqref{eq:et-lag-one-cost} and
\eqref{eq:et-kernel-comparison-costs} prove
\[
\begin{split}
    W_2(\nu K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}},\nu)&\leqslant C\sqrt{dh^3}\,, \qquad
    W_2(\nu(K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})^2,\nu K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})
       \leqslant C\sqrt{dh^5}\,.
\end{split}
\]

Substitute these estimates into \Cref{lem:et-local-framework} and use
\(1-\rho\geqslant h/(2\kappa)\):
\[
    W_2(\widehat\pi_h^{\mathrm{Heun}},\nu)
    \leqslant C\sqrt{dh^3}+C\kappa h^{-1}\sqrt{dh^5}
    \leqslant C\kappa\sqrt{dh^3}\,.
\]
Finally, \(W_2(\nu,\pi)\leqslant\lVert \chi\rVert_{L^2(\pi)}
\leqslant C\sqrt{dh^3}\), so the triangle inequality proves
\eqref{eq:et-direct-invariant-bound}.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
The normalization in \Cref{sec:normalization} turns
\eqref{eq:et-direct-invariant-bound} into
\eqref{eq:et-stationary-upper}.  Existence and uniqueness follow from
\Cref{prop:et-contractivity} and its rescaling.

Contractivity and invariance give, for a universal \(c_0>0\),
\[
    W_2(\mu(K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})^N,
       \widehat\pi_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})
    \leqslant \mathrm{e}^{-c_0N\alpha h}
       W_2(\mu,\widehat\pi_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})\,.
\]
Consequently,
\[
\begin{split}
    W_2(\mu(K_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})^N,\pi)
    &\leqslant \mathrm{e}^{-c_0N\alpha h}\,
       \bigl\{W_2(\mu,\pi)
          +W_2(\pi,\widehat\pi_h^{\mathrm{Heun}})\bigr\}+W_2(\widehat\pi_h^{\mathrm{Heun}},\pi)\,.
\end{split}
\]
Squaring this inequality and applying
\eqref{eq:et-stationary-upper} proves
\eqref{eq:et-finite-time-upper}, after changing the universal constants.

Choose a sufficiently small
universal \(c_1>0\) and set
\[
    \beta h=c_1h_0\min\Bigl\{1,
       \Bigl(\frac{\varepsilon^2}{\kappa d}\Bigr)^{1/3}\Bigr\}\,,
    \qquad
    N=\Bigl\lceil C\kappa\,(\beta h)^{-1}
       \log\Bigl(\mathrm{e}+\frac{\alphaW_2^2(\mu,\pi)}{\varepsilon^2}\Bigr)\Bigr\rceil\,.
\]
The two terms in \eqref{eq:et-finite-time-upper} are then each at most
\(\varepsilon^2/2\), proving the query bound for arbitrary
initialization.  If \(\mu=\delta_{x_\star}\), strong convexity and
integration by parts give
\[
    \alpha\,\mathbb{E}\lVert X-x_\star\rVert^2
    \leqslant\mathbb{E}\langle \nabla V(X),X-x_\star\rangle=d\,,
    \qquad X\sim\pi\,.
\]
For \(0<\varepsilon\leqslant\sqrt d/2\), we have \(d/\varepsilon^2\geqslant4\),
and hence \(\log(\mathrm{e}+d/\varepsilon^2)\leqslant2\log(d/\varepsilon^2)\).
Since
\(\kappa\,(\beta h)^{-1}\lesssim
\kappa+\kappa^{4/3}d^{1/3}\varepsilon^{-2/3}\),
this gives \eqref{eq:et-complexity}.

Each iteration uses two gradient queries, which is absorbed in the
universal constant.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:uld-midpoint-Hminus1-stability}
There is a universal constant \(C>0\) such that, for every
\(r\in\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)\) and \(0\leqslant t\leqslant1\),
\[
    \lVert \mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal L^\dagger} r\rVert_{\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)}
    \leqslant C\,\lVert r\rVert_{\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)}\,.
\]
Moreover, \( (\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal L^\dagger})_{t\ge 0} \) extends to a strongly continuous semigroup on
\(\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)\).
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
First let \(r\coloneqq\delta_{\bm\pi}A\) with \(A\) smooth.  Write
\( (\Phi_t(z))_{t\ge 0} \) for the exact forward underdamped Langevin flow and
\(J_t(z)\coloneqq D_z\Phi_t(z)\), with the Brownian path argument suppressed.
Then,
\[
    D\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal L}\varphi(z)
    =\mathbb{E}\bigl[J_t(z)^\top D\varphi(\Phi_t(z))\bigr]\,.
\]

The drift Jacobian has universally bounded
operator norm because
\(0\preceq\nabla^2V\preceqI_d\), so
\(\lVert J_t\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{Ct}\).
Jensen's inequality and the bound on \(J_t\) give
\[
    \lVert D\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal L}\varphi\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}^2
    \le \mathrm{e}^{2Ct}\,\lVert D\varphi\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}^2\,.
\]
Duality immediately gives
\[
    \lVert \mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal L^\dagger}r\rVert_{\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)}
    \leqslant\mathrm{e}^{Ct}\,\lVert r\rVert_{\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)}\,.
\]

Divergences of smooth compactly supported vector
fields are dense by \eqref{eq:uld-midpoint-Hminus1-variational}.  On these divergences,
\(L^2(\bm\pi)\)-strong continuity and Poincar\'e's inequality imply
strong continuity in \(\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)\).  The displayed bound
extends it to the completion.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-13: body_md -->
\label{lem:uld-midpoint-local-differential-bounds}
With Euclidean norms on phase space, there is a universal constant
\(C>0\) such that, for every \(0<h\leqslant h_0\),
\begin{align}
    \lVert m_h\rVert_2
    &\leqslant C\sqrt d\,h^2\,,
    &
    \lVert D_zm_h\rVert_{L^\infty(\bm\pi;\mathrm{op})}
    &\leqslant Ch\,,
    \label{eq:uld-midpoint-mean-bounds}\\
    \lVert D_zR_h\rVert_{L^2(\Omega;\mathrm{HS})}
    &\leqslant C\sqrt d\,h\,,
    &
    \lVert D_GR_h\rVert_{L^\infty(\Omega;\mathrm{op})}
    &\leqslant Ch^{5/2}\,,
    \notag\\
    \lVert D_GR_h\rVert_{L^2(\Omega;\mathrm{HS})}
    &\leqslant C\sqrt d\,h^{5/2}\,.
    \notag
\end{align}

The full local error also satisfies
\[
    \lVert D_z\mathcal E_h\rVert_{L^\infty(\Omega;\mathrm{op})}
    \leqslant Ch\,.
\]
<!-- end lemma-13 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Stationarity and the adjoint semigroup give
\[
    \mathbb{E}[\nabla V(X_s)\mid Z_h]
    =\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{(h-s)\mathcal L^\dagger}\nabla V\bigr)(Z_h)\,,
    \qquad 0\leqslant s\leqslant h\,.
\]
Apply this conditional expectation to the formulas for
\(\mathcal E_h^x\) and \(\mathcal E_h^p\) in
\eqref{eq:exact-midpoint-defect}.  In those two integrals, write \(s\)
for forward time and then set \(t\coloneqq h-s\).  This gives
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
    m_h
    &=\int_0^hA_t\,\mathrm d t-hA_{h/2}
    =-\int_0^{h/2}t\dot A_t\,\mathrm d t
       +\int_{h/2}^h(h-t)\dot A_t\,\mathrm d t\,,
\end{aligned}
    \label{eq:uld-midpoint-mean-semigroup}
\end{equation}
where
\[
    g_t\coloneqq\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal L^\dagger}(\nabla V)\,,
    \qquad
    A_t\coloneqq(a_tg_t,\mathrm{e}^{-2t}g_t)\,.
\]
The second equality in \eqref{eq:uld-midpoint-mean-semigroup} follows
by integration by parts on \([0,h/2]\) and \([h/2,h]\).
We bound
\(\lVert \dot A_t\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}\) by controlling \(g_t\) and its time derivative.
By \eqref{eq:uld-generators},
\(\mathcal L^\dagger(\nabla V)=-\nabla^2V\,p\), since \(\nabla V\)
depends only on \(x\).  Weighted integration by parts and averaging
\(p\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I_d)\), respectively, give
\[
\begin{aligned}
    \lVert \nabla V\rVert_{L^2(\pi)}^2
    &=\mathbb{E}_\pi\operatorname{tr}(\nabla^2V)\leqslant d\,,
    \qquad \lVert \mathcal L^\dagger(\nabla V)\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}^2
    =\mathbb{E}_\pi\operatorname{tr}\bigl((\nabla^2V)^2\bigr)\leqslant d\,.
\end{aligned}
\]
Applying the chain rule to \(\nabla V\) along the reverse position path
and taking conditional expectations gives, in \(L^2(\bm\pi)\),
\[
    g_t-\nabla V
    =\int_0^t\mathrm{e}^{s\mathcal L^\dagger}
       \mathcal L^\dagger(\nabla V)\,\mathrm d s\,.
\]
The \(L^2\) bounds on \(\nabla V\) and
\(\mathcal L^\dagger(\nabla V)\), together with strong continuity of
\(\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal L^\dagger}\), justify differentiation:
\(\dot g_t=\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal L^\dagger}\mathcal L^\dagger(\nabla V)\).
Contractivity of the semigroup gives
\(\lVert g_t\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}+\lVert \dot g_t\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}\leqslant2\sqrt d\).
Since \(\dot a_t=\mathrm{e}^{-2t}\), the product rule yields
\[
    \dot A_t=\bigl(\mathrm{e}^{-2t}g_t+a_t\dot g_t,\,
       \mathrm{e}^{-2t}\,(\dot g_t-2g_t)\bigr)\,,
    \qquad
    \lVert \dot A_t\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}\leqslant C\sqrt d
    \quad(0\leqslant t\leqslant h\leqslant h_0\leqslant1)\,.
\]
Substituting into \eqref{eq:uld-midpoint-mean-semigroup} gives
\(\lVert m_h\rVert_2\leqslant C\sqrt d\,h^2\), as required in
\eqref{eq:uld-midpoint-mean-bounds}.

We next prove the terminal state derivative bounds for \(m_h\) and
\(R_h\), keeping \(G\) fixed.  Differentiating the SDE for
\((X_{h-t},-P_{h-t})\) with respect to \(z=(x,p)\) gives
\[
\begin{gathered}
    \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d t}
    \begin{bmatrix}D_zX_{h-t}\\-D_zP_{h-t}\end{bmatrix}
    =\begin{bmatrix}
       0&I_d\\
       -\nabla^2V(X_{h-t})&-2I_d
     \end{bmatrix}
     \begin{bmatrix}D_zX_{h-t}\\-D_zP_{h-t}\end{bmatrix}\,,\qquad
    \begin{bmatrix}D_zX_h\\-D_zP_h\end{bmatrix}
    =\begin{bmatrix}I_d&0\\0&-I_d\end{bmatrix}\,.
\end{gathered}
\]
The initial matrix comes from the starting point \((x,-p)\) of the
reversed path after the momentum flip.  Since
\(\lVert \nabla^2V\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant1\), the coefficient matrix
in this variational equation has operator norm at most a universal
constant.  Gr\"{o}nwall's inequality therefore gives, uniformly in \(z\) and \(G\),
\[
    \lVert D_zX_{h-t}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
       +\lVert D_zP_{h-t}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
    \leqslant C\mathrm{e}^{Ct}\leqslant C\,,
    \qquad 0\leqslant t\leqslant h\leqslant h_0\leqslant1\,.
\]
The chain rule then bounds
\(D_z[\nabla V(X_{h-t})]=\nabla^2V(X_{h-t})D_zX_{h-t}\)
in operator norm by \(C\).  Applying this bound to each integral and
midpoint term in \eqref{eq:exact-midpoint-defect}, with \(t\) denoting
reverse time, yields
\[
\begin{aligned}
    \lVert D_z\mathcal E_h^x\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
    &\leqslant C\,\Bigl(\int_0^h a_t\,\mathrm d t+h a_{h/2}\Bigr)
       \leqslant Ch^2\,,\\
    \lVert D_z\mathcal E_h^p\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
    &\leqslant C\,\Bigl(\int_0^h\mathrm{e}^{-2t}\,\mathrm d t+h\mathrm{e}^{-h}\Bigr)
       \leqslant Ch\,.
\end{aligned}
\]
Here we used \(a_t\leqslant t\) and \(\mathrm{e}^{-2t}\leqslant1\).
Combining the two components gives
\(\lVert D_z\mathcal E_h\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant Ch\).
The uniform derivative bound and independence of \(G\) from the
terminal state justify differentiation under \(\mathbb{E}_G\):
\[
    D_zm_h(z)=\mathbb{E}_G[D_z\mathcal E_h(z,G)]\,,
    \qquad
    D_zR_h(z,G)=D_z\mathcal E_h(z,G)-D_zm_h(z)\,.
\]
Consequently,
\[
    \lVert D_zm_h\rVert_{L^\infty(\bm\pi;\mathrm{op})}\leqslant Ch\,,
    \qquad
    \lVert D_zR_h\rVert_{L^\infty(\Omega;\mathrm{op})}\leqslant Ch\,.
\]
Since \(D_zR_h\) is a \(2d\times2d\) matrix, its Hilbert--Schmidt
norm is at most \(\sqrt{2d}\) times its operator norm.  Taking the
\(L^2\) norm over \((Z_h,G)\) therefore gives
\[
    \lVert D_zR_h\rVert_{L^2(\Omega;\mathrm{HS})}
    \leqslant\sqrt{2d}\,
       \lVert D_zR_h\rVert_{L^\infty(\Omega;\mathrm{op})}
    \leqslant C\sqrt d\,h\,.
\]

We now estimate the Malliavin derivative, keeping the terminal state
fixed.  For a unit direction \(f\in\mathcal H\), let
\(\xi_t\coloneqq D_GX_{h-t}f\) and \(\eta_t\coloneqq-D_GP_{h-t}f\).
These variations of the path after the momentum flip satisfy
\[
    \dot\xi_t=\eta_t\,,
    \qquad
    \dot\eta_t=-2\eta_t-\nabla^2V(X_{h-t})\,\xi_t+2f_t\,,
    \qquad
    \xi_0=\eta_0=0\,.
\]
Integrating these equations gives
\(\eta_t=2\int_0^t\mathrm{e}^{-2(t-s)}f_s\,\mathrm d s
-\int_0^t\mathrm{e}^{-2(t-s)}\,\nabla^2V(X_{h-s})\,\xi_s\,\mathrm d s\) and
\(\xi_t=\int_0^t\eta_s\,\mathrm d s\).
Cauchy--Schwarz bounds
\(\lVert 2\int_0^t\mathrm{e}^{-2(t-s)}f_s\,\mathrm d s\rVert\) by \(2\sqrt t\).
For \(0\leqslant t\leqslant h\leqslant h_0\), the integral involving
\(\nabla^2V(X_{h-s})\,\xi_s\) is bounded by
\(Ct^2\sup_{0\leqslant s\leqslant t}\,\lVert \eta_s\rVert\) and can be
absorbed in the estimate for \(\sup_{0\leqslant s\leqslant t}\,\lVert \eta_s\rVert\), giving
\[
    \lVert D_GP_{h-t}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant Ct^{1/2}\,,
    \qquad
    \lVert D_GX_{h-t}\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant Ct^{3/2}\,.
\]
Since \(D_GP_{h-t}\) and \(D_GX_{h-t}\) take values in \(\mathbb{R}^d\),
they have rank at most \(d\), so their Hilbert--Schmidt norms are
at most \(\sqrt d\) times the operator norm bounds above.
Differentiating the formulas for \(\mathcal E_h^p\) and
\(\mathcal E_h^x\) in \eqref{eq:exact-midpoint-defect} gives
\(\lVert D_G\mathcal E_h^p\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant Ch^{5/2}\) and
\(\lVert D_G\mathcal E_h^x\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}\leqslant Ch^{7/2}\).
The extra power of \(h\) in the position component comes from
\(a_t=(1-\mathrm{e}^{-2t})/2\leqslant t\).  Since
\(D_GR_h=D_G\mathcal E_h\), this proves the operator and
Hilbert--Schmidt bounds for \(D_GR_h\).
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-14: body_md -->
\label{lem:uld-midpoint-second-generator}
There is a universal \(C>0\) such that
\[
    \lVert (\mathcal L^\dagger)^2\zeta\rVert_{\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)}
    \leqslant C\sqrt d\,.
\]
<!-- end lemma-14 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
We first bound the generator on functions with bounded momentum
Hermite degree, then apply duality.  Let \(\Pi_{\leqslant r}\) be the
orthogonal projection onto momentum Hermite degrees at most
\(r\in\mathbb N_0\).  Here the momentum Hermite basis
\(H_\ell\) is the normalized basis defined in
\Cref{subsec:tb-malliavin}; the range of \(\Pi_{\leqslant r}\) is the
span of \(M_\ell(x)H_\ell(p)\) with \(\lvert\ell\rvert\leqslant r\).
With the component weighted divergences defined in
\Cref{subsec:uld-normalization},
\[
    \mathcal L
    =\delta_p\nabla_x-\delta_x\nabla_p-2\mathcal A_p\,.
\]
For a smooth function \(\psi\) of momentum degree at most \(r\), the
weighted divergence estimate \eqref{eq:tb-weighted-divergence}, in
the Gaussian and spatial variables respectively, gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
    \lVert \delta_p\nabla_x\psi\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}^2
    &\leqslant(r+1)\,\lVert \nabla_x\psi\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}^2\,,\\
    \lVert \delta_x\nabla_p\psi\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}^2
    &\leqslant r\,\lVert \nabla_x\psi\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}^2
       +\lVert \nabla_p\psi\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}^2\,,\\
    \lVert \mathcal A_p\psi\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}^2
    &\leqslant r\,\lVert \nabla_p\psi\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}^2\,.
\end{aligned}
\]
Indeed, the first two estimates use
\(\lVert \nabla_p\nabla_x\psi\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}^2
\leqslant r\,\lVert \nabla_x\psi\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}^2\), and the last
follows from \(\mathcal A_pH_\ell=\lvert\ell\rvert H_\ell\).
Taking square roots, the triangle inequality bounds
\(\lVert \mathcal L\psi\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}\) by
\((\sqrt{r+1}+\sqrt r)\,\lVert \nabla_x\psi\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}
+(1+2\sqrt r)\,\lVert \nabla_p\psi\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}\).
Since \(\Pi_{\leqslant r}\) contracts the full gradient seminorm,
\[
    \lVert \mathcal L\Pi_{\leqslant r}\varphi\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}
    \leqslant C\sqrt{r+1}\,\lVert D\varphi\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}\,,
\]
where \(C\) is a universal constant.
For \(f\in L^2(\bm\pi)\) of momentum degree at most \(r\), the
generator changes the momentum degree by at most one.  Consequently,
\[
\begin{aligned}
    \bigl\lvert\langle \mathcal L^\dagger f,\varphi\rangle\bigr\rvert
    &=\bigl\lvert\langle f,\mathcal L\Pi_{\leqslant r+1}\varphi\rangle_{L^2(\bm\pi)}\bigr\rvert
    \leqslant C\sqrt{r+2}\,\lVert f\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}\,
       \lVert D\varphi\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}\,.
\end{aligned}
\]
Thus, \(\lVert \mathcal L^\dagger f\rVert_{\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)}
\leqslant C\sqrt{r+2}\,\lVert f\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}\), with the left side
interpreted distributionally.
Apply this estimate with \(r=2\) and
\(f\coloneqq\mathcal L^\dagger\zeta\).  Direct calculation gives
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal L^\dagger\zeta
    =-\bigl\{\langle p,\nabla^2V\,p\rangle
       -\operatorname{tr}(\nabla^2V)\bigr\}
       +\delta_\pi(\nabla V)-2\zeta\,.
    \label{eq:uld-midpoint-first-generator}
\end{equation}
The three terms are Hermite polynomials in momentum of exact degrees \(2\), \(0\), and \(1\), so
they are orthogonal.  Gaussian moments,
\(\lVert \nabla V\rVert_{L^2(\pi)}^2\leqslant d\), and
\eqref{eq:tb-weighted-divergence} give
\[
\begin{aligned}
    \lVert \langle p,\nabla^2V\,p\rangle
       -\operatorname{tr}(\nabla^2V)\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}^2
    &=2\,\lVert \nabla^2V\rVert_{L^2(\pi;\mathrm{HS})}^2\leqslant2d\,,\\
    \lVert \delta_\pi(\nabla V)\rVert_{L^2(\pi)}^2
    &=\lVert \nabla^2V\rVert_{L^2(\pi;\mathrm{HS})}^2
       +\mathbb{E}_\pi\langle \nabla V,\nabla^2V\,\nabla V\rangle\leqslant2d\,,\\
    \lVert \zeta\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}^2
    &=\lVert \nabla V\rVert_{L^2(\pi)}^2\leqslant d\,.
\end{aligned}
\]
It follows that \(\lVert f\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}\leqslant C\sqrt d\), which
proves the lemma by the dual estimate.  Approximation of the spatial
coefficients of \(f\) in \(L^2(\pi)\), keeping their momentum degrees
fixed, extends that estimate to the present \(C^2\) potential.
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- proposition-10: body_md -->
\label{prop:uld-midpoint-weighted-divergence}
There is a universal constant \(C>0\) such that, for
every \(0<h\leqslant h_0\),
\[
    \lVert \delta_{\bm\pi}m_h\rVert_{\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)}
    \leqslant C\sqrt d\,h^3\,.
\]
<!-- end proposition-10 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
We express \(\delta_{\bm\pi}m_h\) as a midpoint quadrature error and separate
the adjoint semigroup contribution from a remainder.
From \eqref{eq:uld-midpoint-mean-semigroup},
\begin{equation}
    \delta_{\bm\pi}m_h=\int_0^h\psi_t\,\mathrm d t-h\psi_{h/2}\,,
    \qquad
    \psi_t\coloneqq a_t\delta_xg_t+\mathrm{e}^{-2t}\delta_pg_t\,.
    \label{eq:uld-midpoint-divergence-quadrature}
\end{equation}
The commutator identities
\begin{align*}
    \delta_x\mathcal L^\dagger F-\mathcal L^\dagger\delta_xF
    &=\delta_p(\nabla^2V\,F)\,, \qquad
    \delta_p\mathcal L^\dagger F-\mathcal L^\dagger\delta_pF
    =-\delta_xF+2\delta_pF
\end{align*}
involve only \(\nabla^2V\).  Combining them with
\(\dot a_t=\mathrm{e}^{-2t}\), \(\frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d t}\,\mathrm{e}^{-2t}=-2\mathrm{e}^{-2t}\), and
\(\dot g_t=\mathcal L^\dagger g_t\) gives
\[
    \dot\psi_t-\mathcal L^\dagger\psi_t
    =a_t\delta_p(\nabla^2V\,g_t)\,,
    \qquad
    \psi_0=\zeta\,.
\]
Hence, in \(\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)\),
\[
    \psi_t=\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal L^\dagger}\zeta+\mathcal R_t\,,
    \qquad
    \mathcal R_t
    \coloneqq\int_0^t\mathrm{e}^{(t-s)\mathcal L^\dagger}
       \bigl\{a_s\delta_p(\nabla^2V\,g_s)\bigr\}\,\mathrm d s\,.
\]
Since
\(\delta_{\bm\pi}(0,\nabla^2V\,g_s)=\delta_p(\nabla^2V\,g_s)\),
\eqref{eq:uld-midpoint-Hminus1-variational} and the
\(L^2(\bm\pi)\) contractivity of the adjoint semigroup give
\[
    \lVert \delta_p(\nabla^2V\,g_s)\rVert_{\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)}
    \leqslant\lVert \nabla^2V\,g_s\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}
    \leqslant\lVert g_s\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}
    \leqslant\lVert \nabla V\rVert_{L^2(\pi)}\leqslant\sqrt d\,.
\]
By \Cref{lem:uld-midpoint-Hminus1-stability} and \(a_s\leqslant s\),
\[
    \lVert \mathcal R_t\rVert_{\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)}
    \leqslant C\sqrt d\,t^2\,.
\]
Integrating the bound on \(\lVert \mathcal R_t\rVert_{\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)}\) over
\([0,h]\) and evaluating it at \(h/2\) gives
\begin{equation*}
    \Bigl\lVert \int_0^h\mathcal R_t\,\mathrm d t-h\mathcal R_{h/2} \Bigr\rVert
       _{\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)}
    \leqslant C\sqrt d\,h^3\,.
\end{equation*}

It remains to bound the contribution of \(\mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal L^\dagger}\zeta\)
to \(\delta_{\bm\pi}m_h\).  We use the second-order integral expansion in
\(\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)\):
\begin{equation}
    \mathrm{e}^{t\mathcal L^\dagger}\zeta
    =\zeta+t\mathcal L^\dagger\zeta
       +\int_0^t(t-s)\mathrm{e}^{s\mathcal L^\dagger}
          (\mathcal L^\dagger)^2\zeta\,\mathrm d s\,.
    \label{eq:uld-midpoint-extrapolation-Duhamel}
\end{equation}
To justify \eqref{eq:uld-midpoint-extrapolation-Duhamel}, first note
that \eqref{eq:uld-midpoint-first-generator} can be written as
\[
 \mathcal L^\dagger\zeta
 =-\{\langle p,\nabla^2V\,p\rangle-\operatorname{tr}(\nabla^2V)\}
       +\delta_\pi(\nabla V)-2\zeta\,.
\]
Gaussian moments and \eqref{eq:tb-weighted-divergence} give
\[
 \lVert \delta_\pi(\nabla V)\rVert_{L^2(\pi)}^2
 =\mathbb{E}_\pi\operatorname{tr}((\nabla^2V)^2)
   +\mathbb{E}_\pi\langle \nabla V,\nabla^2V\nabla V\rangle\leqslant2d\,,
 \qquad \lVert \mathcal L^\dagger\zeta\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}\leqslant C\sqrt d\,.
\]
\Cref{lem:uld-midpoint-second-generator} gives
\((\mathcal L^\dagger)^2\zeta\in\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)\).
Testing against a smooth function in the generator core and
integrating the weak generator identity twice proves the expansion.
The flow derivative bound in the proof of
\Cref{lem:uld-midpoint-Hminus1-stability} controls the gradient of
the forward semigroup in these pairings; approximation in the
generator core extends the identity to \(\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)\).

The bound on \(\mathcal R_t\),
\eqref{eq:uld-midpoint-extrapolation-Duhamel}, and
\Cref{lem:uld-midpoint-Hminus1-stability,lem:uld-midpoint-second-generator}
give the affine approximation
\begin{equation}
    \lVert \psi_t-\zeta-t\mathcal L^\dagger\zeta\rVert
       _{\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)}
    \leqslant C\sqrt d\,t^2\,,
    \qquad 0\leqslant t\leqslant h\,.
    \label{eq:uld-midpoint-affine-remainder}
\end{equation}
The midpoint rule is exact on the affine function
\(t\mapsto\zeta+t\mathcal L^\dagger\zeta\), so subtracting this function
from both the integrand and the midpoint evaluation in
\eqref{eq:uld-midpoint-divergence-quadrature} leaves the error unchanged:
\[
\begin{aligned}
    \delta_{\bm\pi}m_h
    ={}&\int_0^h\bigl(\psi_t-\zeta-t\mathcal L^\dagger\zeta\bigr)
       \,\mathrm d t-h\bigl(\psi_{h/2}-\zeta-\tfrac h2\mathcal L^\dagger\zeta\bigr)\,.
\end{aligned}
\]
Applying \eqref{eq:uld-midpoint-affine-remainder} to these two remainders
and using the triangle inequality gives
\[
    \lVert \delta_{\bm\pi}m_h\rVert_{\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)}
    \leqslant C\sqrt d\,\Bigl(\int_0^h t^2\,\mathrm d t
       +h\,(h/2)^2\Bigr)
    \leqslant C\sqrt d\,h^3\,.\qedhere
\]
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- lemma-15: body_md -->
\label{lem:uld-midpoint-random-displacement}
Let \(\mu\in\mathcal P_2(\mathbb{R}^q)\) have density proportional to
\(\mathrm{e}^{-\mathcal V_\mu}\), where \(\mathcal V_\mu\in C^2(\mathbb{R}^q)\) and
\(\nabla^2\mathcal V_\mu\preceq\LambdaI_q\) for some \(\Lambda>0\).
On a probability space \(\Omega\), let \(Y\sim\mu\) and let \(G\)
be an independent isonormal Gaussian process with separable
Cameron--Martin space \(\mathcal H\).
Let \(U(Y,G)\in L^2(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^q)\) be weakly differentiable in \(Y\)
and Malliavin-differentiable in \(G\), with $D_YU,D_GU\in L^\infty(\Omega;\mathrm{op})$.
Each derivative holds the other variable fixed, and norms over
\(\Omega\) include both variables.  Define
\(m(Y)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}[U\mid Y]\) and \(R\coloneqq U-m(Y)\).  Then,
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
    W_2\bigl(\operatorname{Law}(Y+U),\mu\bigr)
    &\leqslant\lVert \delta_\mu m\rVert_{\dot H^{-1}(\mu)}
       +\frac12\,\lVert D_YU\rVert_{L^\infty(\Omega;\mathrm{op})}\,
          \lVert U\rVert_{L^2(\Omega)}\\
    &\qquad+\frac12\,\lVert D_GU\rVert_{L^\infty(\Omega;\mathrm{op})}\,
       \bigl(\lVert D_YR\rVert_{L^2(\Omega;\mathrm{HS})}^2
          +\Lambda\,\lVert R\rVert_{L^2(\Omega)}^2\bigr)^{1/2}\,.
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:uld-midpoint-random-displacement}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-15 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
We subtract a divergence-free field on the product of the base and
Gaussian spaces from the velocity of the linear interpolation.
Let \(w\) be the orthogonal projection of \(m\) onto the closure of
gradients in \(L^2(\mu;\mathbb{R}^q)\).  By
\Cref{subsec:tb-sobolev},
\[
    \delta_\mu w=\delta_\mu m\,,
    \qquad
    \lVert w\rVert_{L^2(\mu)}=\lVert \delta_\mu m\rVert_{\dot H^{-1}(\mu)}\,.
\]
The weighted divergence estimate \eqref{eq:tb-weighted-divergence}
in the base variable gives
\[
    \lVert \delta_\mu R\rVert_{L^2(\Omega)}^2
    \leqslant\lVert D_YR\rVert_{L^2(\Omega;\mathrm{HS})}^2
       +\Lambda\,\lVert R\rVert_{L^2(\Omega)}^2\,.
\]
Here \(\delta_\mu R\) is defined by closure from smooth fields.
Since \(\mathbb{E}[R\mid Y]=0\), it also integrates to zero in the Gaussian
variable for almost every \(Y\).  Write \(\delta_G\) for the adjoint
of \(D_G\).  The Gaussian spectral gap and the inverse on functions
that integrate to zero, recalled in \Cref{subsec:tb-malliavin}, give
\[
\begin{gathered}
    F\coloneqq-D_G(-\mathcal L_{\mathrm{OU}})^{-1}\delta_\mu R\,,
    \qquad \delta_GF=-\delta_\mu R\,,
    \qquad \lVert F\rVert_{L^2(\Omega;\mathcal H)}
    \leqslant\lVert \delta_\mu R\rVert_{L^2(\Omega)}\,.
\end{gathered}
\]
Thus, the product field \((U-w,F)\) has zero weighted divergence:
\[
    \delta_\mu(U-w)+\delta_GF
    =\delta_\mu(m-w)+\delta_\mu R-\delta_\mu R=0\,.
\]
In this formula and below, \(w\) is evaluated at \(Y\).
Set \(\mathsf X_\theta\coloneqq Y+\theta U\), for \(0\leqslant\theta\leqslant1\).
For a smooth compactly supported test function \(\varphi\), test the
preceding divergence identity against
\(\varphi_\theta(Y,G)\coloneqq\varphi(Y+\theta U)\).  By the adjoint definitions of the
divergences,
\[
    0=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\langle D_Y\varphi_\theta,U-w\rangle
       +\langle D_G\varphi_\theta,F\rangle_{\mathcal H}\bigr]\,.
\]
The chain rule then gives
\[
    0=\mathbb{E}\langle D\varphi(\mathsf X_\theta),(I_q+\theta D_YU)\,(U-w)+\theta D_GU\,F\rangle\,.
\]
Subtracting this identity from the derivative of
\(\mathbb{E}\varphi(\mathsf X_\theta)\) gives
\[
    \frac{\mathrm d}{\mathrm d\theta}\,\mathbb{E}\varphi(\mathsf X_\theta)
    =\mathbb{E}\langle D\varphi(\mathsf X_\theta),w-\theta D_YU\,(U-w)-\theta D_GU\,F\rangle\,.
\]
Consequently, a velocity for the continuity equation is
\[
    v_\theta(\mathsf X_\theta)\coloneqq
       \mathbb{E}\bigl[w-\theta D_YU\,(U-w)-\theta D_GU\,F\mid \mathsf X_\theta\bigr]\,.
\]
Projection and conditional centering give
\[
    \lVert U-w\rVert_{L^2(\Omega)}^2
    =\lVert U\rVert_{L^2(\Omega)}^2-\lVert w\rVert_{L^2(\mu)}^2
    \leqslant\lVert U\rVert_{L^2(\Omega)}^2\,.
\]
Conditional Jensen's inequality therefore bounds the velocity by
\[
\begin{aligned}
    \lVert v_\theta\rVert_{L^2(\operatorname{Law}(\mathsf X_\theta))}
    &\leqslant\lVert w\rVert_{L^2(\mu)}
       +\theta\,\lVert D_YU\rVert_{L^\infty(\Omega;\mathrm{op})}\,
          \lVert U\rVert_{L^2(\Omega)}
    +\theta\,\lVert D_GU\rVert_{L^\infty(\Omega;\mathrm{op})}\,
       \lVert F\rVert_{L^2(\Omega;\mathcal H)}\,.
\end{aligned}
\]
The metric speed bound
\cite[Theorem~8.3.1]{AmbrosioGigliSavare2008}, integrated over
\([0,1]\), proves \eqref{eq:uld-midpoint-random-displacement}.
All integration by parts identities extend to the stated weak
derivatives by the Sobolev chain rule and closure: the composition
\(\varphi(Y+\theta U)\) has square integrable derivatives in both variables,
and \(F\) belongs to the domain of \(\delta_G\).
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
The proof of \Cref{lem:uld-midpoint-random-displacement} recovers and
strengthens \cite[Lemma~4.11]{HigherOrderRHMC}.  In its notation, let
\(Z\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I_m)\) and \(G\sim\mathsf{N}(0,I_n)\) be independent,
let \(f(z,g)\) have Lipschitz constants \(A\) in \(z\) and \(B\) in
\(g\), and write \(b(z)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_Gf(z,G)\) and
\(R\coloneqq f(Z,G)-b(Z)\).  Suppose \(R\) takes values in a fixed
subspace \(E\subseteq\mathbb{R}^m\), with orthogonal projection \(\Pi_E\),
and let \(L\) be a linear map into a Hilbert space.  Then
\[
\begin{aligned}
    &W_2\bigl(\operatorname{Law}(L\,(Z+f(Z,G))),\operatorname{Law}(L\,(Z+b(Z)))\bigr)
    \leqslant\sqrt m\,\Bigl\{
       \frac12\,\lVert L\Pi_E\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}B^2
       +\frac32\,\lVert L\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}AB\Bigr\}\,.
\end{aligned}
\]
This improves the coefficient \(5\) of the mixed term in that lemma
to \(3/2\).  Indeed, use the field \(F\) from the preceding proof
with Gaussian measure \(\mu=\mathsf{N}(0,I_m)\), and interpolate by
\(\mathsf X_\theta\coloneqq Z+b(Z)+\theta R\), for \(0\leqslant\theta\leqslant1\).
Integration by parts with the divergence-free field
\((R,F)\) allows us to subtract
\(D_Z(L\mathsf X_\theta)R+D_G(L\mathsf X_\theta)F\) from the original velocity \(LR\)
without changing the continuity equation.  The chain rule gives the
resulting velocity, before conditioning on \(L\mathsf X_\theta\):
\[
    -L\,(Db+\theta D_zR)\,R-\theta LD_GR\,F\,.
\]
Since \(Db+\theta D_zR=(1-\theta)\,Db+\theta D_zf\), its operator norm is at most
\(A\).  Gaussian Poincar\'e and conditional variance give, on a joint probability space $\Omega$ for \((Z,G)\),
\[
    \lVert R\rVert_{L^2(\Omega)}\leqslant\sqrt m\,B\,,
    \qquad
    \lVert D_zR\rVert_{L^2(\Omega;\mathrm{HS})}\leqslant\sqrt m\,A\,,
    \qquad
    \lVert F\rVert_{L^2(\Omega;\mathbb{R}^n)}\leqslant\sqrt m\,\sqrt{A^2+B^2}\,.
\]
Also, \(D_GR\) takes values in \(E\), so
\(\lVert LD_GR\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}
\leqslant\lVert L\Pi_E\rVert_{\mathrm{op}}B\).
Integrating the velocity bound and using
\(\sqrt{A^2+B^2}\leqslant A+B\) proves the claim.
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Use \Cref{lem:uld-midpoint-random-displacement} with
\(Y=Z_h\), \(U=\mathcal E_h\), \(\mu=\bm\pi\), and \(\Lambda=1\).
Here the base potential on phase space is
\(\mathcal V_{\bm\pi}(x,p)=V(x)+\lVert p\rVert^2/2\), whose Hessian is
bounded above by \(I_{2d}\).

We have
\(D_z\mathcal E_h=D_zm_h+D_zR_h\).  The first bound below is proved
directly for this full derivative in
\Cref{lem:uld-midpoint-local-differential-bounds}.
Since \(m_h(z)\) is independent of \(G\), we have \(D_Gm_h=0\) and
\(D_G\mathcal E_h=D_GR_h\), so the second bound follows from the
Gaussian derivative estimate in the same lemma.  Thus,

\[
    \lVert D_z\mathcal E_h\rVert_{L^\infty(\Omega;\mathrm{op})}
    \leqslant Ch\,,
    \qquad
    \lVert D_G\mathcal E_h\rVert_{L^\infty(\Omega;\mathrm{op})}
    \leqslant Ch^{5/2}\,.
\]
Gaussian Poincar\'e at fixed \(Z_h\), followed by the
Hilbert--Schmidt derivative estimate, yields
\[
    \lVert R_h\rVert_{L^2(\Omega)}
    \leqslant\lVert D_GR_h\rVert_{L^2(\Omega;\mathrm{HS})}
    \leqslant C\sqrt d\,h^{5/2}\,.
\]
Together with \eqref{eq:uld-midpoint-mean-bounds} and conditional
centering, this gives
\[
    \lVert \mathcal E_h\rVert_{L^2(\Omega)}^2
    =\lVert m_h\rVert_{L^2(\bm\pi)}^2+\lVert R_h\rVert_{L^2(\Omega)}^2
    \leqslant Cd h^4\,.
\]
Finally,
\(\lVert D_zR_h\rVert_{L^2(\Omega;\mathrm{HS})}\leqslant C\sqrt d\,h\)
by \Cref{lem:uld-midpoint-local-differential-bounds}, and
\(\lVert \delta_{\bm\pi}m_h\rVert_{\dot H^{-1}(\bm\pi)}
\leqslant C\sqrt d\,h^3\) by
\Cref{prop:uld-midpoint-weighted-divergence}.  Substitution into
\eqref{eq:uld-midpoint-random-displacement} gives directly
\[
